A tree–free approach to 3D Yang–Mills Langevin dynamic. Analytic estimates and the existence of a model for a regularity structure
A. Sevostyanov

Abstract
. Using the multi–index approach to regularity structures due to F. Otto et al., we construct a regularity structure and a model for it associated to the stochastic Langevin equation for the 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills functional. For the model we also obtain global stochastic and global pointwise weighted Besov type estimates which hold almost surely. The model is defined as a limit of a sequence of smooth models introduced with the help of a mollified noise. When the mollification is removed the sequence converges in a certain topology defined with the help of the stochastic estimates. To obtain these results we develop the multi–index approach for systems of equations with vector–valued white noises. This project is motivated by the problem for constructing 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure and by the earlier results of the author on the related problem of canonical quantization of the Yang–Mills field on the Minkowski space.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Canonical quantization of the Yang–Mills field on the Minkowski space and 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure

It is well known that the Yang–Mills theory on the Minkowski space is the main ingredient of the most complete contemporary model in quantum field theory, called the Standard Model, which describes weak, strong and electromagnetic interactions of elementary particles. The problem for defining a mathematically rigorous quantum Yang–Mills theory remains open for many decades, and became central in quantum field theory at least since the Standard Model was introduced in the end of 1960s. One of the aspects of this problem, a rigorous definition of the quantized Yang–Mills Hamiltonian and the proof of the mass gap property for it, is stated as a Clay Mathematical Institute Millennium problem (see
[
31
]
).

In paper
[
43
]
it was shown that a natural candidate for the canonically quantized Hamiltonian of the Yang–Mills field on the Minkowski space is the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck operator associated to the yet to be defined 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure (see
[
21
]
, Chapter 3 for the Hamiltonian formulation for the Yang–Mills field on the Minkowski space).

Actually, in
[
43
]
it was observed that the classical Hamiltonian of the Yang–Mills field on the trivial
K
–bundle over the Minkowski space associated to the adjoint representation of a compact Lie group
K
with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{k}}
belongs to a class of Hamiltonians which can be described in the toy finite–dimensional case as follows.

Let
M
be a Riemannian manifold with a metric
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. Consider a Hamiltonian on the symplectic manifold
T^{*}M
of the form

(1.1.1)

\frac{1}{2}(\langle p,p\rangle+\langle v(x),v(x)\rangle),

where
p\in T_{x}M\simeq T^{*}_{x}M
is the momentum and
v={\rm grad}~\phi
is a potential vector field. According to the results of
[
43
]
, this Hamiltonian has a family of canonical quantizations of the form

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a=1}^{n}\xi_{a}^{*}(x)\xi_{a}(x):L_{2}(M,d\mu)\rightarrow L_{2}(M,d\mu),

where
\xi_{a}(x)
,
a=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}~M
is an orthonormal basis of
T_{x}M
, and
\xi_{a}^{*}(x)
is the operator formally adjoint to
\xi_{a}(x)
with respect to the canonical scalar product in the space
L_{2}(M,d\mu)
of square integrable functions on
M
with respect to the measure
d\mu=\psi e^{-2\phi}dx
, where
dx
is the Lebesgue measure on
M
associated to the Riemannian metric, and
\psi
is an arbitrary smooth non-vanishing function on
M
.

The function
\psi
is a quantization parameter which can be chosen arbitrary if we do not require that the quantized components of the momentum in the Hamiltonian formalism should be self–adjoint in
L_{2}(M,d\mu)
. This restriction can be often lifted in quantum field theory when
M
is infinite–dimensional and the Hamiltonian formalism momentum
p
has no physical meaning, hence its quantized components do not need to be self–adjoint (do not confuse
p
with the relativistic momentum which is, of course, a physical observable).

On the other hand, the possibility of introducing an extra factor
\psi
in the definition of the measure
d\mu
becomes primarily important in quantum field theory because the usual Lebesgue measure does not exist on infinite–dimensional spaces, but choosing
\psi
appropriately one can expect to find proper probabilistic counterparts of measures of the type
d\mu=\psi e^{-2\phi}dx
which can be defined on such spaces.

The key observation of
[
43
]
is that the Hamiltonian of the Yang–Mills field on the Minkowski space is of type (
1.1.1
), where
M
is the space of gauge equivalence classes of connections
A
on the trivial
K
–bundle over
\mathbb{R}^{3}
associated to the adjoint representation of
K
, and
\phi
is the Chern–Simons functional which we denote by
CS
. A natural metric on
M
is induced by the natural metric
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on the space of
{\mathfrak{k}}
-valued differential one–forms on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, see formula (
1.2.1
) below.

The discussion in this introduction is rather informal, and we do not rigorously define the space of gauge equivalence classes of connections on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
required for quantization. This is a subtle matter as probabilistic measures on infinite–dimensional function spaces are typically supported on spaces which consist of very singular generalized functions (see
[
10
,
11
]
for a rigorous definition of such spaces in the case of gauge equivalence classes of connections on a two–dimensional or a three–dimensional torus).

A natural choice of the function
\psi
for which the measure
d\mu
is expected to exist in the case of the Yang–Mills field is such that

(1.1.2)

\psi e^{-2\phi}=\exp(-YM(A)-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\langle(A-Z),(A-Z)\rangle),

and hence

\psi=\exp(-YM(A)-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\langle(A-Z),(A-Z)\rangle+2CS(A)),

where
YM(A)
is the Euclidean Yang–Mills functional on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see formula (
1.2.4
) below),
m>0
is a parameter, and we identify the affine space of connections with the space of
{\mathfrak{k}}
–valued one–forms on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by fixing an origin
Z
, so that the expression
\langle(A-Z),(A-Z)\rangle
is gauge invariant
1
1

1

In
[
43
]
a slightly different choice of
\psi
is used. The choice of
\psi
as in (
1.1.2
) is more natural and symmetric, especially in the case of non–abelian
K
.
.

As explained in
[
43
]
, the necessity for introducing a non–zero parameter
m
in the right hand side of formula (
1.1.2
) for the “density function” of the corresponding conjectural measure
d\mu
, called the 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure, is already clear in the abelian case when
K=U(1)
, and in this case the corresponding measure can be constructed by classical methods of functional analysis. In fact, for
K=U(1)
the expression in the exponent in the right hand side of (
1.1.2
) is quadratic in
A
, and the gauge action is reduced to adding differentials of functions, so that one can fix a “gauge” (e.g the Coulomb gauge as in
[
43
]
) which provides a model for the space of gauge equivalence classes of connections, and make the 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure manifestly Gaussian on this model. Note that the corresponding quantization of the Yang–Mills Hamiltonian in this case, given by the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck operator associated to this Gaussian measure, is different from that used in quantum electrodynamics.

As shown in
[
43
]
, this operator exhibits one important property which is also expected from the quantized self–adjoint Yang–Mills Hamiltonian in the non–abelian case and which is primarily important for applications in quantum chromodynamics: this quantized Hamiltonian has a gap in the spectrum which separates the zero eigenvalue corresponding to the ground state from the rest of the spectrum.

As it is clear from the preceding discussion the problem for quantizing of the classical Hamiltonian of the Yang–Mills field defined on the Minkowski space is now reduced to the problem for defining the 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure.
In the next part of the Introduction we shall discuss this problem in more detail.

1.2.
3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure and the Langevin equation associated to the 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills functional

Firstly, we recall some basic facts from gauge theory.
Let
K
be a compact semi-simple Lie group,
{\mathfrak{k}}
its Lie algebra and
\mathfrak{g}
the
complexification of
{\mathfrak{k}}
. We denote by
(\cdot,\cdot)
the Killing form of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Recall that the restriction of this form to
{\mathfrak{k}}
is non-degenerate and negatively defined. For the Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
on
{\mathfrak{k}}
it will be important to consider a family of Lie brackets
g[\cdot,\cdot]
on the vector space
{\mathfrak{k}}
, and the corresponding family of Lie brackets on
\mathfrak{g}
, parametrized by
g\in\mathbb{R}
which is called the coupling constant. We shall denote the vector space
{\mathfrak{k}}
equipped with the Lie bracket corresponding to
g
by
{\mathfrak{k}}_{g}
, and the corresponding compact semi-simple Lie group by
K_{g}
.

We shall consider the Yang–Mills functional on the affine space of smooth
connections on the trivial
K_{g}
-bundle over the standard Euclidean space

{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and associated to the adjoint representation of
K_{g}
. Fixing a trivialization of this bundle and the
trivial connection as an origin in the affine space of connections we can identify this space
with the space
\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g})
of
{\mathfrak{k}}_{g}
-valued 1-forms on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.
Let
A
be such a connection.

Let
\Omega^{*}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g})
be the space of

{\mathfrak{k}}
-valued differential forms on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We
define a scalar product on this space, whenever it is finite, by

(1.2.1)

\langle\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\omega_{1}\wedge,*\omega_{2})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}*(\omega_{1}\wedge,*\omega_{2})d^{3}x,~\omega_{1,2}\in\Omega^{*}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g})

where
*
stands for the Hodge star operation associated to a standard
Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and we evaluate the Killing form
(\cdot,\cdot)
on the
values of
\omega_{1}
and
*\omega_{2}
and also take their exterior product. Fix orthonormal Cartesian coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
corresponding to the Euclidean metric.

We recall that the covariant derivative

d_{A}:\Omega^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g})\rightarrow\Omega^{n+1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g})
associated to
A

is defined by
d_{A}\omega=d\omega+g[A\wedge\omega]
, and the operator
d_{A}^{*}

formally adjoint to
d_{A}
with respect to scalar product (
1.2.1
) is
equal to
-*d_{A}*
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{g}
be the corresponding gauge group, i.e. the group
of
K_{g}
-valued smooth maps
{\hat{g}}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow K_{g}
. The Lie algebra of
\mathcal{K}_{g}

is isomorphic to the space
\Omega^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g})
of
{\mathfrak{k}}_{g}
-valued smooth functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

The gauge group
\mathcal{K}_{g}
acts on the space of connections
by

(1.2.2)

{\hat{g}}\circ A=-d{\hat{g}}{\hat{g}}^{-1}+{\hat{g}}A{\hat{g}}^{-1},~{\hat{g}}\in\mathcal{K}_{g},~A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g}),

where we denote
d{\hat{g}}{\hat{g}}^{-1}={\hat{g}}^{*}\theta_{R}
,
{\hat{g}}A{\hat{g}}^{-1}=\mathrm{Ad}{\hat{g}}(A)
, and

\theta_{R}
is the right-invariant Maurer–Cartan form on
K_{g}
. The corresponding vector fields for the action of the Lie algebra
\Omega^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g})
of
\mathcal{K}_{g}
have the form

(1.2.3)

X_{A}=-dX+g[X,A]=-d_{A}X,~X\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g}),~A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g}),

where
X_{A}=-d_{A}X\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g})
should be regarded as a tangent vector to the space of connections at point
A
, and the tangent space is naturally identified with
\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g})
.

Denote by
F_{A}
the curvature 2-form of this connection,
F_{A}=dA+\frac{g}{2}[A\wedge A]
.
Here as usual we denote by
[A\wedge A]
the
operation which takes the
exterior product of
{\mathfrak{k}}
-valued 1-forms and the commutator of their values in

{\mathfrak{k}}
. The Yang–Mills functional
YM
evaluated at
A
is defined by
the formula

(1.2.4)

YM=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(F_{A}\wedge,*F_{A}),

where we evaluate the Killing form on the
values of
F
and
*F
and also take their exterior product.

The Yang–Mills functional can be compactly rewritten in terms of scalar product (
1.2.1
),

YM=\frac{1}{2}\langle F_{A},F_{A}\rangle.

According to
[
40
]
a probabilistic measure with the “density function” given by (
1.1.2
) can be sought as an invariant measure for the Markov process constructed with the help of the Langevin equation associated to the functional

-YM(A)-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\langle(A-Z),(A-Z)\rangle

which appears in the exponent in formula (
1.1.2
).

The Langevin equation associated to this functional is the gradient flow equation with an extra white noise term. Formally it has the form

(1.2.5)

\partial_{t}A=-d_{A}^{*}F_{A}-m^{2}(A-Z)+\xi,

where
\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\xi_{i}dx_{i}
,
\xi_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
are independent white noises on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
with values in the adjoint representation of
K_{g}
, with the covariance induced by the Killing form
(\cdot,\cdot)
,
t
, called “time”, is a coordinate on
\mathbb{R}
in the product
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is the fixed Cartesian coordinate system on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in it.

Recall that we fixed a trivialization of the trivial
K_{g}
–bundle over
\mathbb{R}^{3}
associated to the adjoint representation and an origin in the affine space of connections. We can assume that it is done in such a way that
Z=0
after identification of the affine space of connections with
\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},{\mathfrak{k}}_{g})
. Then equation (
1.2.5
) takes the form

(1.2.6)

\partial_{t}A=-d_{A}^{*}F_{A}-m^{2}A+\xi,

One of the problems which arise in the course of the study of equation (
1.2.6
) is that the partial differential operator applied to
A
in the right hand side is not elliptic. To overcome this difficulty one adds to the right hand side of (
1.2.6
) an extra term of the form
d_{A}H(A)
, where
H(A)
is a sufficiently regular
{\mathfrak{k}}_{g}
–valued functional of
A
,

(1.2.7)

\partial_{t}A=-d_{A}^{*}F_{A}+d_{A}H(A)-m^{2}A+\xi,

Since
d_{A}H(A)
has the form of an infinitesimal gauge transformation (
1.2.3
) with
X=H(A)
, at least formally solutions of equations (
1.2.5
) and (
1.2.7
) are gauge equivalent in law under time-dependent gauge transformations (see e.g.
[
11
,
15
]
).

A common choice of
H(A)
, that we are going to use, is
H(A)=-d^{*}A
(Coulomb gauge) which yields the so-called DeTruck-Zwanziger term
-d_{A}d^{*}A
(see
[
18
,
49
]
) in the right hand side of (
1.2.7
). With this choice of
H(A)
equation (
1.2.7
) takes the form

(1.2.8)

\partial_{t}A=-d_{A}^{*}F_{A}-d_{A}d^{*}A-m^{2}A+\xi,

or, in terms of the coordinates
(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
and of the corresponding components of
A=\sum_{i=1}^{3}A_{i}dx_{i}
,

(1.2.9)

\partial_{t}A_{i}=\Delta A_{i}-m^{2}A_{i}+g[A_{j},2\partial_{j}A_{i}-\partial_{i}A_{j}+g[A_{j},A_{i}]]+\xi_{i},~i=1,2,3,

where
\Delta=\partial_{i}\partial_{i}
is the Laplace operator, and here and below we take sums over all possible values of repeated indices in products.

Equation (
1.2.9
) is highly singular due to presence of the non-linear and of the white noise terms in the right hand side. In its original formal form it has no solutions of any reasonable class.

Now we discuss this difficulty in more detail. Firstly, it well known that the white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
belongs almost surely to a weighted Besov space. Therefore, it suffices to consider equation (
1.2.8
) with
\xi
scalar components of which belong to this space, and hence we can assume that these components are less singular than generic elements of the white noise space.

More precisely, let
\mathcal{D}=C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
be the space of smooth compactly supported real–valued functions on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
the corresponding space of distributions, i.e. the strong dual of
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{S}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
the Schwartz space of smooth real–valued functions on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
decreasing at infinity faster than any polynomial,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
the space of tempered distributions, i.e. its topological dual. For a compact subset
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, let
C^{\infty}({\mathfrak{K}})
be the space of smooth real–valued functions on
{\mathfrak{K}}
,
\mathcal{D}({\mathfrak{K}})=C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathfrak{K}})
the space of smooth real–valued functions supported on
{\mathfrak{K}}
.

We fix a centered Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
which equips it with the structure of a white noise space. Then for any
s<-\frac{5}{2}
scalar components of the white noise term in equation (
1.2.8
) belong almost surely to the weighted Besov spaces
B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}(w)
,
w(x)=(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})^{\frac{5}{2}}
,
x=(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
\left\|x\right\|:=\sqrt{t^{2}+x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}}
, with exponent
s
associated to a non-Euclidean scaling of
\mathbb{R}^{4}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see
[
25
]
, Lemma 2.23). In Section
6
we shall prove a more general result for recentered maps of a model for a regularity structure.

For completeness we recall the definition of weighted Besov spaces.
For
x=(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
, let
|x|:=\sqrt[4]{t^{2}+x_{1}^{4}+x_{2}^{4}+x_{3}^{4}}
,
B_{R}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:|x|\leq R\}
,
R>0
.
For any real–valued function
\varphi(x)
,
x=(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
we define
\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(t^{\prime},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=\lambda^{-d}\varphi(\lambda^{-2}(t-t^{\prime}),\lambda^{-1}(x_{1}-y_{1}),\lambda^{-1}(x_{2}-y_{2}),\lambda^{-1}(x_{3}-y_{3}))
, where
d=5
is the parabolic dimension of
\mathbb{R}^{4}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Let
s<0
and fix
r\in\mathbb{N}:=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
,
r>|s|
. Then the Besov space
B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}(w)
can be defined as the space of tempered distributions
\zeta\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
satisfying the following condition

(1.2.10)

|\zeta(\varphi_{x}^{\lambda})|\lesssim w(x)\lambda^{-s}

which holds uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}:=\{\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(B_{1}):\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{r}}\leq 1\}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<\lambda\leq 1
.

Here and below the symbol
C\lesssim D
for any two real–valued quantities (i.e. real numbers or real–valued functions) means that
C\leq aD
, where
a\geq 0
. When using the symbol
C\lesssim D
we often specify if the constant
a
in the inequality depends on the variables on which
C
and
D
depend. For instance, in (
1.2.10
) the constant in the inequality only depends on
\zeta
.

The first natural idea in the course of the study of equation (
1.2.8
) is to replace the singular white noise term with a mollification
\xi^{\rho}(x):=\xi(\eta_{x}^{\rho})
,
\rho>0
, where
\eta\in\mathcal{S}
satisfies
\eta(y)\geq 0
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\eta(y)dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|\eta(y)|dy=1
, and by
\xi(\eta_{x}^{\rho})
we mean that the scalar components of
\xi
are evaluated at
\eta_{x}^{\rho}
. To preserve the symmetries of equation (
1.2.8
) with respect to spacial reflections we shall also require that
\eta(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is even in the spacial coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
.

However, it turns out that in the limit
\rho\to 0
, when the components of
\xi^{\rho}
tend to the corresponding components of
\xi
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, solutions to the regularized equation (
1.2.8
) with
\xi^{\rho}
instead of
\xi
in the right hand side have no limits in any relevant topology, even for
\xi
with components in the spaces
B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}(w)
,
s<-\frac{5}{2}
.

To circumvent this obstruction a more deep modification of equation (
1.2.8
) is required which is achieved by adding to the right hand side extra terms, called counter terms, depending on
\rho
. A proper realization of this idea initiated by M. Hairer in pioneering paper
[
27
]
for a class of non-linear stochastic differential equations and developed in
[
8
,
9
,
12
]
is very complex.

Firstly, equations of this class, called subcritical, with mollified white noises, can be lifted to equations in spaces of the so-called modelled distributions which are functions with values in other spaces called model spaces of regularity structures. The lifted equations can be solved and pushed down to equations in Besov spaces using a reconstruction operator defined in terms of smooth models for regularity structures. The smooth models depend on the mollification parameter
\rho
and one shows that there is an appropriate choice of a smooth model such that when
\rho\to 0
the pushed down solutions converge in a reasonable sense. However, the pushed down “renormalized” equations, which are the original equations with mollified noises and extra counter terms, often have no meaning in the limit
\rho\to 0
since coefficients in the counter terms may diverge when
\rho\to 0
.

Note that the method suggested in
[
8
,
9
,
12
,
27
]
yields a local existence theorem for the Cauchy problem, where
t
plays the role of time, similar to Picard’s theorem for the local existence and uniqueness of solutions to ordinary differential equations.

This method was applied in
[
10
]
to equation (
1.2.8
) with the spatial part defined on the three–dimensional torus (see also
[
11
]
for the case of the two–dimensional torus). Note that
m=0
in this case. The corresponding counter term obtained in
[
10
,
11
]
, that should be added to the right hand side of equation (
1.2.8
) with a mollified white noise, has the form
cA
, where
c
is a constant which depends on
\rho
.

Note that the mollified noise
\xi^{\rho}
is not manifestly gauge covariant. But the gauge covariance can be recovered in the limit
\rho\to 0
, at least in law.

The renormalized equation (
1.2.8
) with a mollified white noise has the form

(1.2.11)

\partial_{t}A+m^{2}A=-d_{A}^{*}F_{A}-d_{A}d^{*}A+cA+\xi^{\rho},

or, in terms of the coordinates,

(1.2.12)

(\partial_{t}-\Delta+m^{2})A_{i}=g[A_{j},2\partial_{j}A_{i}-\partial_{i}A_{j}+g[A_{j},A_{i}]]+cA_{i}+\xi_{i}^{\rho},~i=1,2,3.

In papers
[
4
,
5
,
35
,
36
,
37
,
38
,
39
,
44
]
an alternative multi–index approach to regularity structures and to their applications for solving non-linear parabolic stochastic partial differential equations was developed. This approach has several benefits.

Firstly, the multi–index combinatorics is much simpler that the combinatorics of colored threes used in
[
8
,
9
,
12
]
. Secondly, in the multi–index framework one can obtain solutions to the corresponding stochastic differential equations using a priori estimates and the continuity method (see
[
4
]
).

In the case of Yang-Mills measures Hairer’s approach yields local in time solutions to the corresponding renormalized equation (
1.2.11
), with the spatial part defined on the two–dimensional or the three–dimensional torus, and
m=0
, as described in
[
10
]
and
[
11
]
. In the two–dimensional case the Yang-Mills measure obtained long time ago by other means (see
[
34
,
42
]
or
[
20
,
24
]
for earlier works) is an invariant measure for the corresponding Markov process defined on a space of gauge equivalence classes of connections (see
[
14
]
). But the construction of global solutions to equation (
1.2.11
), and of the corresponding Yang-Mills measure in the three–dimensional case are still cornerstone open problems.

The purpose of the project initiated in this paper is to develop the multi–index approach for solving equation (
1.2.11
). In particular, in this paper we construct a regularity structure associated to equation (
1.2.11
), prove the existence of a model for it, and obtain stochastic and pointwise estimates for the model. For this purpose we develop the multi–index approach for systems of equations and vector–valued noises.

From now on we only consider equation (
1.2.11
) associated to the Yang-Mills finctional on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We mainly follow the multi–index approach as it is presented in
[
5
]
, and refer the reader to
[
5
]
for motivations of the definitions given in this paper for equation (
1.2.11
).

According to
[
5
]
, the renormalization parameter
c
in equation (
1.2.11
) should be a polynomial in the coupling constant
g
without constant term. The constant
m
is a free parameter of the theory. As we mentioned in the first part of the Introduction, for the existence of the Yang-Mills measure in the abelian case on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
when
{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathbb{R}
,
g=0
, and the polynomial
c
reduces to zero,
m^{2}
must be strictly positive (see also
[
30
]
, Example 1.3 and Remark 1.16 in
[
15
]
). Therefore we naturally assume that in the non–Abelien case
m>0
is a constant independent of
\rho
, and
c
is a polynomial in
g
without constant term the coefficients of which depend on
\rho
.

Equation (
1.2.12
) is our main object of study. Note that the operator
L:=\partial_{t}-\Delta+m^{2}
, which appears in its right hand side, has much better properties than the heat operator commonly used in both Hairer’s and the multi–index approaches. In particular, at infinity its Green function behaves like a function from the Schwartz space.

Note that
m
plays the role of a mass parameter in the theory, and it is expected that the condition
m>0
ensures convergence of the corresponding Markov process on a space of gauge equivalence classes of connections, existence of an invariant Yang–Mills measure for it, and the spectral gap for its generator which should play the role of the Hamiltonian of the canonically quantized Yang–Mills theory on the Minkowski space as shown in
[
43
]
. The last fact explains the mass gap phenomenon in quantum chromodynamics.

In conclusion we briefly describe the structure of the paper. More comments on the content can be found in the beginning of each section.

In Section
2
, after preliminary discussion of scaling properties of functions from Besov spaces and of some properties of the operator
L
, we introduce the model space and the smooth model for a regularity structure associated to equation (
1.2.12
). We follow the philosophy of the multi–index approach in the form described in
[
5
]
for scalar equations. But our definition of the model space adapted for systems of equations with vector–valued noises requires vector–valued analogues of multi–indices which appear in Section
2.3
.

The definition of the recentered maps and of the canonical lift for a smooth model of the regularity structure in Section
2.4
is given by applying a combination of the original Hairer’s method from
[
27
]
and of the multi–index approach, the combinatorics being close to the multi–index approach, and the analytic formulas resemble Hairer’s definition based on the notion of regularizing kernels. The reason for this combined approach originates in the fact that we prefer the simplified multi–index combinatorics but consider the case when
\xi
is a white noise, which is important for preserving gauge covariance, while in
[
5
]
the case of less rough noises is considered. The last fact allows to introduce renentered maps using the heat kernel itself, not its cut–off version, called a regularizing kernel, as in
[
29
]
.

In turn, this possibility is based on the scaling properties of noises. White noises scale (in law) with rational exponents under dilations on the underlying space
\mathbb{R}^{4}
which a priori forces to use estimates for the regularizing kernel in Hölder spaces with integer exponents in the theory of regularity structures. But actually such estimates can only be obtained in Zygmund spaces which are different from Hölder spaces at integer exponents.

One way to circumvent this obstruction is to manually distort the exponents in the definition of regularity structures which is done in the original Hairer’s approach. In the setting of this paper the need for such distortion is already clear from the fact that the components of the white noise
\xi
belong to the spaces
B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}(w)
with
s<-\frac{5}{2}
. This method is applicable in the case of white noises, while the other way is to consider less rough noises which already scale in law with irrational exponents, as it is done in
[
5
]
.

Since gauge covariance, which is only preserved in the case of the white noise, is primarily important for our purposes we follow the former path. Its disadvantage is that in
[
5
]
uniform estimates at all scales are obtained for the model, while in the original Hairer’s approach estimates are local and the scaling parameter takes values in a bounded interval, the last restriction being impossible to overcome in the case of white noises. Since we stick to Hairer’s definition of the recentered maps in its analytic part, we still use a cut–off version of the kernel of the Green function of the operator
L
in this definition. We already mentioned that this Green function behaves like a Schwartz function at infinity unlike the heat kernel itself which is even not integrable. Nevertheless, the Green function of
L
is not compactly supported which makes it unsuitable for Hairer’s definition of recentered maps.

The structure group maps are defined in Section
2.6
with the help of automorphisms relating the recentered maps and the canonical lift which appear in Section
2.5
. The definition of these automorphisms is simple and explicit. Note that the multi–index description of the regularity structure is dual in a sense to the one in Hairer’s approach, and the definition of automorphisms relating the recentered maps and the canonical lift is obtained by “dualizing” a similar definition in terms of regularity structures where it becomes inductive and rather inexplicit (see
[
27
]
, §8.2 or
[
16
]
, Section 2). Formulas of the same type also appeared in
[
36
,
37
]
.

In Section
2.7
we also show, following Hairer’s approach, how to construct a natural lift of equation (
1.2.12
) to an equation in a space of modelled distributions.

Section
3
contains analytic tools required for obtaining stochastic estimates for models. After introducing some function spaces and recalling some classical Sobolev type inequalities we state Schauder estimates for regularizing kernels and the reconstruction theorem for germs in Section
3.2
. We need stochastic pointed and local versions of the Schauder estimates which are close to those obtained in
[
6
]
in the deterministic case (see also
[
22
]
, Chapter 14). A stochastic version of Hairer’s reconstruction theorem for germs is stated in a form close to Theorem 3.2 in
[
7
]
(see also
[
13
]
). The stochastic versions of these two statements are required in the multi–index approach to stochastic estimates for models as suggested in
[
5
,
35
]
where their global stochastic versions are used. Section
3.3
also contains necessarily results on Malliavin derivatives of the recentered maps used in the proof of the stochastic estimates in the multi–index approach.

Section
4
is central in this paper. After recalling in Section
4.1
the so–called generalized BPHZ condition used to fix the renormalization parameter
c(\rho)
we confirm the translational invariance in law for the recentered and for the structure group maps in Section
4.2
and proceed with the statement of the stochastic estimates for them in Section
4.3
.

The proof of these estimates presented in Sections
4.4
,
4.5
,
4.6
,
4.7
,
4.8
and
4.9
is quite lengthy. We proceed by induction over the indices from the index set of the regularity structure following the idea of
[
5
,
35
]
. However, in contrast to
[
5
,
35
]
, we linearly order the arguments in the proof. The other difference is that, since we use local and pointed Schauder estimates for a regularizing kernel on a bounded scale, which naturally appears in a definition of the relevant Hölder spaces, the range of the scaling parameter shrinks after each iteration of the induction step, and to run the induction step again this parameter must be greater than one. As a consequence, starting from a large enough range for the rescaling parameter in the base case, we can obtain stochastic estimates for recentered maps and for structure group maps labeled by indices from the index set of the regularity structure uniformly bounded by a given constant which can be chosen arbitrary large. The BPHZ conditions in our approach should also be fixed at each iteration of the induction step separately for recentered maps labeled by the corresponding indices from the index set.

In Section
5
we show that the smooth models converge in a certain space when the mollification of the white noise is removed, i.e. when
\rho\to 0
. The proof is based, as in
[
25
,
44
]
, on a Cauchy property for smooth models which is established in the same manner as the stochastic estimates.

Finally, in Section
6
using Kolmogorov continuity arguments we obtain weighted Besov type global pointwise estimates for models which hold almost surely. This is done in Theorem
6.2.1
for the structure group maps of the model and in Theorem
6.3.1
for the recentered maps.

Acknowledgements.
The author is grateful to Thierry Lévy and to Ilya Chevyrev for stimulating discussions at the initial stage of this project.

2.
A regularity structure and a model for the Langevin equation with mollified noise

In this section we introduce a regularity structure and a smooth model for it associated to equation (
1.2.12
). We start by defining the underlying combinatorics relying on the notion of multi–indices which appear in Section
2.1
. In the definition of the smooth model for the regularity structure we explicitly use the kernel of the heat operator with an extra mass term the properties of which are recalled in Section
2.2
. Then we proceed with an algebraic description of the model space in Section
2.3
. Using this model space recentered maps of a smooth model for the corresponding regularity structure and a canonical lift for it associated to equation (
1.2.12
) are defined in Section
2.4
. In the algebraic part of their definition in Lemma
2.4.1
we follow the strategy of
[
5
]
. But, as we mentioned in the introduction, analytic formulas (
2.4.9
) for the recentered maps have some similarity with the original Hairer’s definition given in
[
30
]
as well as provisional local analytic estimates for them in the end of Section
2.4
. The structure group maps of the smooth model are defined with the help of model space automorphisms relating the recentered maps and the canonical lift as in the original Hairer’s approach. However, we introduce these automorphisms in Section
2.5
using the multi–index combinatorics in terms of which their definitions are much more simple and explicit. The definition of the structure group automorphisms resembles Hairer’s definition and their properties derived in Lemma
2.6.1
are similar to those obtained in
[
5
]
in the case of a single scalar equation. In the end of this section we explicitly relate the constructed smooth model to equation (
1.2.12
). To this end we define in Section
2.7
a lift of equation (
1.2.12
) to an equation for modelled distributions and show that after applying the so-called reconstruction operator this equation is reduced to equation (
1.2.12
).

2.1.
Scale invariance and homogeneity

Recall that our final goal is to obtain the
\rho\to 0
limit of solutions to equation (
1.2.11
) with
\xi\in B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}(w)
,
s<-\frac{5}{2}
. The structure of the left hand side of equation (
1.2.12
) implies that such solutions should belong at least to the local version of the space
B^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}
,
\alpha:=s+2
.
Therefore, following
[
5
]
, in view of definition (
1.2.10
) in terms of a scaling, it is natural to consider scaling properties of equation (
1.2.11
) with respect to the following transformation

(2.1.1)

\xi(x)\mapsto\lambda^{-(\alpha-2)}\xi(R_{\lambda}x),\rho\mapsto\lambda^{-1}\rho,A\mapsto\lambda^{-\alpha}A(R_{\lambda}x),~\lambda\in\mathbb{R},~\lambda>0,

where for
x=(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
, we define
R_{\lambda}x=(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x_{1},\lambda x_{2},\lambda x_{3})
. From now on we also assume that
\alpha=-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
, where
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
,
\varepsilon\not\in\mathbb{Q}
can be chosen arbitrary small.

Denoting
x_{0}:=t
and introducing multi–indices
{\bf n}=(n_{0},n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
corresponding to the coordinates
(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
we arrive at the following induced transformation for partial derivatives

(2.1.2)

\partial^{{\bf n}}A_{i}(x)\mapsto\lambda^{|{{\bf n}}|-\alpha}\partial^{{\bf n}}A_{i}(R_{\lambda}x),~i=1,2,3,

where for
{{\bf n}}=(n_{0},n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
one defines
\partial^{{\bf n}}=\prod_{i=0}^{3}\partial_{i}^{n_{i}}
, and

(2.1.3)

|{{\bf n}}|=2n_{0}+n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}.

Equation (
1.2.11
) becomes invariant under transformations (
2.1.1
), (
2.1.2
) if one postulates that these transformations act on
g
,
m^{2}
and
c
as follows

(2.1.4)

g\mapsto\lambda^{\alpha+1}g,~m^{2}\mapsto\lambda^{2}m^{2},~c\mapsto\lambda^{2}c.

The relevant combinatorics can be presented in the most convenient way using the set of multi–indices
{\bf Mult}
over the set
\{{{\bf g}}\}\coprod\mathbb{N}^{4}
, where
{\bf g}
is a formal variable. The multi–indices are functions
\beta:\{{{\bf g}}\}\coprod\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
taking non–zero values only at finitely–many elements of
\{{{\bf g}}\}\coprod\mathbb{N}^{4}
. Let
{\bf 0}=(0,0,0,0)\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, and denote by
\delta_{{\bf g}}
(resp.
\delta_{{\bf n}}
) the multi–index equal to 1 at
{{\bf g}}
(resp.
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
) and zero otherwise.

For any multi–index
\beta
we introduce its homogeneity
|\beta|
by

(2.1.5)

|\beta|=(\alpha+1)\beta({{\bf g}})+\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}})(|{{\bf n}}|-\alpha)+\alpha.

Then writing

(2.1.6)

c=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\beta=k\delta_{{\bf g}}+\delta_{\bf 0}\\
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}\end{array}}c_{\beta}g^{\beta({{\bf g}})}

for some
c_{\beta}=c_{\beta}(\rho)\in\mathbb{R}
we arrive at the following transformation low for
c_{\beta}
induced by (
2.1.4
)

(2.1.7)

c_{\beta}\mapsto\lambda^{2-|\beta|}c_{\beta}.

This transformation rule and (
2.1.1
) imply that if we fix
\lambda^{-1}\rho=1
and consider the limit
\rho\to 0
removing the regularization of
\xi
then
c_{\beta}(\rho)\to\infty
for
|\beta|<2
and
c_{\beta}(\rho)\to 0
for
|\beta|>2
(note that for non-zero
c_{\beta}
one has
|\beta|\not\in\mathbb{Q}
). Therefore we can assume that
c_{\beta}=0
for
|\beta|\geq 2
. As by (
2.1.5
)
|k\delta_{{\bf g}}+\delta_{\bf 0}|=k(\alpha+1)
, one has from (
2.1.6
)

c=\sum_{0<k<\frac{2}{1+\alpha}}c_{k}g^{k},~c_{k}=c_{k}(\rho)\in\mathbb{R},

and under the assumption
\alpha=-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
, where
0<\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{10}
,
\varepsilon\not\in\mathbb{Q}
, we infer

\frac{2}{1+\alpha}=\frac{4}{1-2{\varepsilon}}\leq 5,

and hence

c=\sum_{k=1}^{4}c_{k}g^{k}.

From now on we shall always assume that
\varepsilon\not\in\mathbb{Q}
and satisfies

(2.1.8)

0<\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{100}.

2.2.
The heat operator with a mass term

To introduce the smooth model for a regularity structure associated to equation (
1.2.12
) we shall need some properties of the operator
L=\partial_{t}-\Delta+m^{2}
which appears in its left hand side. These properties are well known.

For
x=(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
, denote
\bar{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let

\|\bar{x}\|=\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}}

be the standard norm on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The Green function
K_{m}
of the operator
L
can be obtained using the Fourier transform. It has the form

(2.2.1)

K_{m}(x)=\frac{\theta(t)}{(4\pi t)^{\frac{3}{2}}}e^{-\frac{\|\bar{x}\|^{2}}{4t}-m^{2}t}=K_{H}(x)e^{-m^{2}t},

where

\theta(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&{\rm if}~t>0\\
0&{\rm if}~t\leq 0\end{array},\right.

and
K_{H}(x)=\frac{\theta(t)}{(4\pi t)^{\frac{3}{2}}}e^{-\frac{\|\bar{x}\|^{2}}{4t}}
is the heat kernel.

For
x=(x_{0},x_{1},x_{3},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
let
\|x\|:=\sqrt{x_{0}^{2}+x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}}
be the standard Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. We also denote by
|x|
the parabolic distance from
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
to the origin defined by
|x|=\sqrt[4]{x_{0}^{2}+x_{1}^{4}+x_{2}^{4}+x_{3}^{4}}
. The parabolic metric
d(x,y):=|x-y|
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
is a genuine metric.

For any
R>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
we denote by
B_{R}(x):=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:|x-y|\leq R\}
the parabolic ball of radius
R
centered at
x
. We shall also write for brevity
B_{R}:=B_{R}(0)
.

Fix a function
\varsigma\in\mathcal{D}
supported in
B_{1}
, and let
K(x):=K_{m}(x)\varsigma(x)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\{0\}
. Then
K
is a 2-regularizing kernel according to Definition 14.9 in
[
22
]
.

Denote the corresponding integral operator by
\mathcal{K}
,

(2.2.2)

\mathcal{K}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}K(x-y)f(y)dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}K(y)f(x-y)dy.

Let also
\mathcal{K}_{m}
be the integral operator with the kernel
K_{m}
,

\mathcal{K}_{m}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}K_{m}(x-y)f(y)dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}K_{m}(y)f(x-y)dy.

Many functions that we shall introduce in the next section will belong to the space
\mathcal{O}
of smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
of polynomial growth which is defined by

\mathcal{O}=\{f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4}):~\forall~{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}~\exists~N({{\bf n}}),C({{\bf n}})\geq 0:~|\partial^{{\bf n}}f(x)|\leq C({{\bf n}})(1+\|x\|^{2})^{N({{\bf n}})}\}.

Tempered distributions have the following useful property (see Theorem IX.4(a) in
[
41
]
).

Lemma 2.2.1
.

For any
\xi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
\eta\in\mathcal{S}
one has
(\xi*\eta)(x):=\xi(\eta^{1}_{x})\in\mathcal{O}
. In particular, for any
\rho>0
, one has
\xi^{\rho}\in\mathcal{O}
.

Now we show that the operator
\mathcal{K}
acts in the space
\mathcal{O}
.

For any
{{\bf n}}=(n_{0},n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
and
x=(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
denote
x^{{{\bf n}}}=x_{0}^{n_{0}}x_{1}^{n_{1}}x_{2}^{n_{2}}x_{3}^{n_{3}}
. We call
|{{\bf n}}|
the parabolic degree of the monomial
x^{{\bf n}}
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
\mathbb{P}_{n}\subset\mathcal{O}
be the subspace generated by monomials of parabolic degrees up to
n
. We call
\mathbb{P}_{n}
the space of polynomials of parabolic degree at most
n
.

We proceed by observing that, since
K
is compactly supported and absolutely integrable, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
the space
\mathbb{P}_{n}
and the space of polynomials of degree at most
n
are invariant under the action of the operator
\mathcal{K}
. We state this obvious fact as a lemma for future references.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

(i) Let
P
be a polynomial on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
of parabolic degree at most
n
. Then
\mathcal{K}P
is well defined and is a polynomial of parabolic degree at most
n
.

(ii) Let
P
be a polynomial on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
of degree at most
n
. Then
\mathcal{K}P
is well defined and is a polynomial of degree at most
n
.

As a corollary of this lemma we immediately obtain the following result.

Proposition 2.2.3
.

\mathcal{K}
is well defined as an operator
\mathcal{K}:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

First recall that
K
is compactly supported and absolutely integrable. Hence for any
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
one has
\mathcal{K}f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, and for any
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}

\partial^{{\bf n}}(\mathcal{K}f)(x)=(\mathcal{K}\partial^{{\bf n}}f)(x).

In particular, for any
f\in\mathcal{O}
, one has
\mathcal{K}f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, and for any
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
we have using Lemma
2.2.2
(ii)

|\partial^{{\bf n}}(\mathcal{K}f)(x)|=|(\mathcal{K}\partial^{{\bf n}}f)(x)|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}K(y)|\partial^{{\bf n}}f(x-y)|\leq

\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}K(y)C({{\bf n}})(1+\|x-y\|^{2})^{N({{\bf n}})}\leq C^{\prime}({{\bf n}})(1+\|x\|^{2})^{N({{\bf n}})}

for some
C({{\bf n}}),C^{\prime}({{\bf n}}),N({{\bf n}})\geq 0
. Therefore by the definition
\mathcal{K}f\in\mathcal{O}
.

∎

2.3.
The index set and the model space for a regularity structure

In this section we introduce the model space for a regularity structure for equation (
1.2.12
). The motivation for this definition will be clarified in the next two sections.

Fix a total normal order on the set multi–indices
\mathbb{N}^{4}
(e.g. one can use the lexicographic order), and for
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
let
W_{{\bf n}}:={\mathfrak{k}}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
{\rm Sym}_{{\bf n}}
the symmetric algebra of
W_{{\bf n}}
, and
{\rm Sym}_{{\bf n}}^{*}
the symmetric algebra of
W_{{\bf n}}^{*}
. The tensor products of algebras

{\rm Sym}_{{{\bf n}}_{0}}\otimes{\rm Sym}_{{{\bf n}}_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes{\rm Sym}_{{{\bf n}}_{p}},

{\rm Sym}_{{{\bf n}}_{0}}^{*}\otimes{\rm Sym}_{{{\bf n}}_{1}}^{*}\otimes\ldots\otimes{\rm Sym}_{{{\bf n}}_{p}}^{*},

where
{{\bf n}}_{0}<{{\bf n}}_{1}<\ldots<{{\bf n}}_{p}<\ldots
is the fixed normal order on
\mathbb{N}^{4}
, form direct systems of algebras with respect to natural inclusions. Denote their direct limits by
{\rm Sym}
and
{\rm Sym}^{*}
, respectively.
{\rm Sym}
and
{\rm Sym}^{*}
are associative algebras elements of which can be regarded as linear combinations of formal infinite tensor products of the form

v_{0}\otimes v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{p}\otimes 1\otimes 1\otimes\ldots,

v_{0}^{*}\otimes v_{1}^{*}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{p}^{*}\otimes 1\otimes 1\otimes\ldots,

respectively, where
v_{i}\in{\rm Sym}_{{{\bf n}}_{i}}
,
v_{i}^{*}\in{\rm Sym}_{{{\bf n}}_{i}}^{*}
,
i=0,\ldots,p
,
p\in\mathbb{N}
, and only finitely many terms in the tensor products are different from 1.

For any multi–index
\beta=l\delta_{\bf g}+\sum_{i=0}^{p}k_{i}\delta_{{{\bf n}}_{i}}
denote

W_{\beta}:=W_{{{\bf n}}_{0}}^{\odot k_{0}}\otimes W_{{{\bf n}}_{1}}^{\odot k_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes W_{{{\bf n}}_{p}}^{\odot k_{p}}\subset{\rm Sym},

W_{\beta}^{*}:={W_{{{\bf n}}_{0}}^{*}}^{\odot k_{0}}\otimes{W_{{{\bf n}}_{1}}^{*}}^{\odot k_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes{W_{{{\bf n}}_{p}}^{*}}^{\odot k_{p}}\subset{\rm Sym}^{*},

where
\odot
stands for the symmetrized tensor power. Note that according to this definition for
l\in\mathbb{N}
one has
W_{l\delta_{\bf g}}=\mathbb{R}
, and

W_{\beta}^{*}
is naturally dual to
W_{\beta}
. This gives rise to a natural pairing

(2.3.1)

{\rm Sym}^{*}\otimes{\rm Sym}\to\mathbb{R},

and for any multi–indices
\beta
and
\gamma
the multiplications in
{\rm Sym}
and in
{\rm Sym}^{*}
give rise to linear maps

(2.3.2)

W_{\beta}\otimes W_{\gamma}\to W_{\beta+\gamma},

(2.3.3)

W_{\beta}^{*}\otimes W_{\gamma}^{*}\to W_{\beta+\gamma}^{*}

which are isomorphisms by the count of dimensions.

Let
V={\mathfrak{k}}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and

V_{\beta}=V_{\beta}^{-}:=W_{\beta}^{*}\otimes V\simeq{\rm Hom}(W_{\beta},V).

We have natural evaluation maps

V_{\beta}\otimes W_{\beta}\to V,~V_{\beta}^{-}\otimes W_{\beta}\to V.

For any multi–index
\beta
we introduce
[\beta]\in\mathbb{Z}
by

(2.3.4)

[\beta]=\beta({\bf g})-\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}}).

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
be the subset of multi–indices
\beta
which either satisfy the condition
[\beta]\geq 0
or have one of the following two forms:

(2.3.5)

\beta=\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}},~{{\bf n}},{{\bf n}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},

(2.3.6)

\beta=2\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}},~{{\bf n}},{{\bf n}}^{\prime},{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}.

Following the terminology of
[
5
]
we call multi–indices of the form
\delta_{{\bf n}}
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
purely polynomial. Denote by
\mathcal{M}_{pp}:=\{\delta_{{\bf n}}:{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}\}
the subset of purely polynomial multi–indices. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}:=\{\beta\in{\bf Mult}:[\beta]\geq 0\}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{M}:=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp}
. As in
[
5
]
, multi–indices from the set
\mathcal{M}
are called populated.

In the following lemma we obtain the local finitness property for the index set corresponding to
\mathcal{M}
in the sense of Hairer.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

The subset
H
of
\mathbb{R}
of the homogeneities
|\beta|
of all possible multi–indices
\beta
and the set
I:=\{|\beta|:\beta\in\mathcal{M}\}\subset H\subset\mathbb{R}
are subsets of the set
(\alpha+1)\mathbb{N}+\mathbb{N}-\alpha\mathbb{N}+\alpha
. In particular,
H
and
I
are locally finite, i.e. any bounded open subinterval of
\mathbb{R}
contains finite numbers of elements of both
H
and of
I
.
\alpha
is the minimal element for both
H
and
I
. In particular, for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
the set
\{\beta\in{\bf Mult}:|\beta|\leq r\}
is finite. Moreover,
\beta=0
is the only multi–index for which
|\beta|=\alpha
.

Proof.

Formula (
2.1.5
) implies that the set
H
is a subset of the set
(\alpha+1)\mathbb{N}+\mathbb{N}-\alpha\mathbb{N}+\alpha
. Since
\alpha+1
and
-\alpha
are strictly positive, we deduce from formula (
2.1.5
) that
\alpha
is the minimal element of
H
and of
I
, and
\beta=0
is the only multi–index for which
|\beta|=\alpha
.

For the same reason for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
the set
((\alpha+1)\mathbb{N}+\mathbb{N}-\alpha\mathbb{N}+\alpha)\cap(-\infty,r)
is finite. Since
H\subset(\alpha+1)\mathbb{N}+\mathbb{N}-\alpha\mathbb{N}+\alpha
, we infer that
H
is locally finite, and hence
I\subset H
is locally finite as well.

∎

Let

T=\bigoplus_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}V_{\beta}^{*}\oplus\bigoplus_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}{V_{\beta}^{-}}^{*}.

T
is called the model space for a regularity structure for equation (
1.2.12
). This choice of
T
is not unique. Another possible choice would be

T^{\prime}:=\bigoplus_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}W_{\beta}\oplus\bigoplus_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}W_{\beta}

(compare with a similar discussion in
[
8
,
9
]
), so that
T\simeq T^{\prime}\otimes V^{*}
. Our choice is convenient for the description of the recentered maps of a smooth model in the next section. However, the structure group maps of the smooth model defined in Section
2.6
are induced by endomorphisms of
T^{\prime}
.

In the notation of
[
29
]
the index set for
T
is
I\cup(I-2)
, so
T=\bigoplus_{r\in I\cup(I-2)}T_{r}
, and for
r\in I\cup(I-2)
the corresponding graded component of
T
has the form

T_{r}=\bigoplus_{\beta\in\mathcal{M},|\beta|=r}V_{\beta}^{*}\oplus\bigoplus_{\beta\in\mathcal{M},|\beta|-2=r}{V_{\beta}^{-}}^{*},

and

\bigoplus_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}V_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}^{*}

is the polynomial sector of
T
.

Since the set
I
is locally finite and bounded from below with the minimal element
\alpha
, the index set
I\cup(I-2)
is locally finite and bounded from below with the minimal element
s=\alpha-2
. Hence
I\cup(I-2)
satisfies the conditions in the definition of index sets in
[
29
]
. Moreover,
V_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}^{*}\simeq{\rm Hom}(V,V)
for all
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
which agrees with the fact that for systems of equations with vector–valued noises the polynomial sector of the regularity structure should be extended accordingly.

2.4.
Recentered maps of the smooth model and the canonical lift in the case of the mollified noise

Following the strategy of
[
5
]
we proceed with defining a smooth model for the regularity structure
T
. Firstly, we introduce an equation for defining recentered maps of the model using induction over the homogeneity. For this purpose we rewrite equation (
1.2.11
) in a convenient form.

For smooth
V
–valued functions
U,U^{\prime},U^{\prime\prime}\in C^{\infty}(V):=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\otimes V
define
\mathcal{A}(U,U^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{B}(U,U^{\prime},U^{\prime\prime})\in C^{\infty}(V)
by

(2.4.1)

\mathcal{A}(U,U^{\prime})=A^{ijk}_{l}[U_{j},\partial_{i}U_{k}^{\prime}]e^{l},~\mathcal{B}(U,U^{\prime},U^{\prime\prime})=B_{l}^{ijk}[U_{j}[U_{k}^{\prime},U_{i}^{\prime\prime}]]e^{l},

where

A^{ijk}_{l}=2\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}-\delta_{il}\delta_{jk},~B_{l}^{ijk}=\delta_{il}\delta_{jk},

\delta_{ij}
is the Kroneker delta,
e^{l}
,
l=1,2,3
is the orthonormal basis of the Cartesian coordinate system in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
fixed in Section
1.2
,
U_{i},U_{i}^{\prime},U_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\otimes{\mathfrak{k}}
,
i=1,2,3
are the components of
U,U^{\prime},U^{\prime\prime}\in C^{\infty}(V)=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\otimes{\mathfrak{k}}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
with respect to this basis, so
U=(U_{1},U_{2},U_{3})
, etc., the commutators of the components
U_{i},U_{i}^{\prime},U_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\otimes{\mathfrak{k}}
,
i=1,2,3
in (
2.4.1
) are taken in
{\mathfrak{k}}
, and the usual product of functions is taken in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
.
The partial derivatives
\partial_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
in (
2.4.1
) act on the factor
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
in the tensor product
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\otimes{\mathfrak{k}}
.

Note that
A\in C^{\infty}(V)
in (
1.2.11
), and the definitions of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
imply that equation (
1.2.11
) can be rewritten in the form

(2.4.2)

LA=g\mathcal{A}(A,A)+g^{2}\mathcal{B}(A,A,A)+cA+\xi^{\rho},

where the operator
L
acts on the factor
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
in the tensor product
C^{\infty}(V)=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\otimes V
.

Let
\mathcal{O}(V):=\mathcal{O}\otimes V
, and for any multi–index
\beta
, let
\mathcal{O}_{\beta}:=\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\beta}\simeq\mathcal{O}\otimes W_{\beta}^{*}\otimes V\simeq\mathcal{O}(V)\otimes W_{\beta}^{*}
,

\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}:=\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}\simeq\mathcal{O}\otimes W_{\beta}^{*}\otimes V\simeq\mathcal{O}(V)\otimes W_{\beta}^{*}
. By this definition
\mathcal{O}_{\beta}=\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
, but it will be convenient to use different symbols for the same space in different contexts.

Since
\mathcal{O}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
one has
\mathcal{O}(V)\subset C^{\infty}(V)
, and for
U,U^{\prime},U^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{O}(V)
one has
\mathcal{A}(U,U^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{B}(U,U^{\prime},U^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{O}(V)
as
\mathcal{O}
is closed under differentiation and multiplication.

Now assume that for all purely polynomial multi–indices
\delta_{{\bf n}}
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
elements

(2.4.3)

\Phi_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}\in\mathbb{P}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}:=\mathbb{P}_{|{{\bf n}}|}\otimes V_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}

are fixed.

Let

(2.4.4)

\Phi_{0}^{-}:=\xi^{\rho}\in\mathcal{O}(V)\simeq\mathcal{O}_{0}^{-},~\Phi_{0}:=\mathcal{K}\Phi_{0}^{-}=\mathcal{K}\xi^{\rho}\in\mathcal{O}_{0}^{-}=\mathcal{O}_{0}.

Here and below the operator
\mathcal{K}
acts on the
\mathcal{O}
–factor in any tensor product
\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}=\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
.

Suppose that for a multi–index
\beta\neq 0
elements
\Phi_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
for
|\gamma|<|\beta|
are given. Then introduce
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
by

(2.4.5)

\Phi_{\beta}^{-}=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in{\bf Mult}:\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\delta_{\bf g}=\beta\end{array}}\mathcal{A}(\Phi_{\beta_{1}},\Phi_{\beta_{2}})+

+\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}\in{\bf Mult}:\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}+2\delta_{\bf g}=\beta\end{array}}\mathcal{B}(\Phi_{\beta_{1}},\Phi_{\beta_{2}},\Phi_{\beta_{3}})+\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}k\in\{1,2,3,4\},\beta_{1}\in{\bf Mult}:\\
k\delta_{\bf g}+\beta_{1}=\beta\end{array}}c_{k}\Phi_{\beta_{1}},

where we assume that
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}=0
if all sums in the right hand side are void,
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are evaluated on the
\mathcal{O}(V)
–factors of the elements
\Phi_{\beta_{i}}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta_{i}}\simeq\mathcal{O}(V)\otimes W_{\beta_{i}}^{*}
,
i=1,2,3
, and product (
2.3.3
) is applied to the
W_{\beta_{i}}^{*}
–factors of these elements.

Correctness of this inductive definition is confirmed in the following lemma along with some other important properties of
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}
.

Lemma 2.4.1
.

Assume that for a multi–index
\beta
elements
\Phi_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
for
|\gamma|<|\beta|
are given. Then the following statements are true.

(i) Formula (
2.4.5
) defines an element
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
, i.e. all sums in the right hand side of (
2.4.5
) are finite, and only
\Phi_{\gamma}
with
|\gamma|<|\beta|
appear in the right hand side of (
2.4.5
). Moreover,
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}=0
if
\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
.

(ii) Assume that
\Phi_{\gamma}=0
for
\gamma\not\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp}
with
|\gamma|<|\beta|
. Then
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}=0
if
\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

(iii) Under the assumption in part (ii), if
[\beta]<0
then
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}=0
if
\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}
, and
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}\in\mathbb{P}_{|\beta|-2}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
.

(iv) Assume that
\Phi_{\gamma}=0
for
\gamma
with
[\gamma]<-1
and
|\gamma|<|\beta|
. Then
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}=0
if
[\beta]<-1
.

Proof.

(i) All sums in the right hand side of (
2.4.5
) are finite since the set of multi–indices is the free commutative monoid generated by the elements
\delta_{\bf g}
and
\delta_{{\bf n}}
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, so every its element is represented as a sum of other elements in finitely many ways.

By (
2.1.5
) for
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\delta_{\bf g}
(resp.
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}+2\delta_{\bf g}
, and
\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+\beta_{1}
,
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
) one has

|\beta|=|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+1
(resp.
|\beta|=|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+|\beta_{3}|+2
, and
|\beta|=k(\alpha+1)+|\beta_{1}|
,
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
,
\alpha+1>0
). Hence only
\Phi_{\gamma}
with
|\gamma|<|\beta|
appear in the right hand side of (
2.4.5
), and
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}=0
if
\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
as
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\delta_{\bf g}
,
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}+2\delta_{\bf g}
, and
\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+\beta_{1}
,
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
which appear in the sums in the right hand side of (
2.4.5
) are not purely polynomial.

(ii) For all
\beta_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
in the right hand side of (
2.4.5
) we have either
[\beta_{i}]\geq 0
or
\beta_{i}=\delta_{{\bf n}}
for some
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, and in the latter case
[\beta_{i}]=-1
.

For a sum
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\delta_{\bf g}
(resp.
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}+2\delta_{\bf g}
, and
\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+\beta_{1}
,
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
) one has by definition (
2.3.4
) that
[\beta]=[\beta_{1}]+[\beta_{2}]+1
(resp.
[\beta]=[\beta_{1}]+[\beta_{2}]+[\beta_{3}]+2
, and
[\beta]=k+[\beta_{1}]
,
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
). Therefore if for at least one
\beta_{i}
in the first two sums
[\beta_{i}]\geq 0
then
[\beta]\geq 0
, and in the last sum we always have
[\beta]\geq 0
.

Otherwise,
\beta=\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{\bf g}
for some
{{\bf n}},{{\bf n}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
(resp.
\beta=\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}}+2\delta_{\bf g}
for some
{{\bf n}},{{\bf n}}^{\prime},{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
), and in all cases
\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

(iii) From the last paragraph of the proof of part (ii) we deduce that
\beta=\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{\bf g}
for some
{{\bf n}},{{\bf n}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
or
\beta=\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}}+2\delta_{\bf g}
for some
{{\bf n}},{{\bf n}}^{\prime},{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
. From formula (
2.1.5
) we obtain that for
\beta=\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{\bf g}
one has
|\beta|=|{{\bf n}}|+|{{\bf n}}^{\prime}|+1
, and for
\beta=\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}}+2\delta_{\bf g}
one has
|\beta|=|{{\bf n}}|+|{{\bf n}}^{\prime}|+|{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}|+2
. In particular,

(2.4.6)

|{{\bf n}}|+|{{\bf n}}^{\prime}|=|\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{\bf g}|-1=|\beta|-1

in the former case, and

(2.4.7)

|{{\bf n}}|+|{{\bf n}}^{\prime}|+|{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}|=|\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}}+2\delta_{\bf g}|-2=|\beta|-2

in the latter case.

Since for any
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
one has
\Phi_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}\in\mathbb{P}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}=\mathbb{P}_{|{{\bf n}}|}\otimes V_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}
, for
\beta=\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{\bf g}
the terms in the first sum in the right hand side of (
2.4.5
) belong to
\mathbb{P}_{|{{\bf n}}|+|{{\bf n}}^{\prime}|-1}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}
, and for
\beta=\delta_{{\bf n}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime}}+\delta_{{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}}+2\delta_{\bf g}
the terms in the second sum in the right hand side of (
2.4.5
) belong to
\mathbb{P}_{|{{\bf n}}|+|{{\bf n}}^{\prime}|+|{{\bf n}}^{\prime\prime}|}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}
. By (
2.4.6
) and (
2.4.7
) this implies
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}\in\mathbb{P}_{|\beta|-2}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
.

(iv) For all
\beta_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
in the right hand side of (
2.4.5
) we have
[\beta_{i}]\geq-1
.

For a sum
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\delta_{\bf g}
(resp.
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}+2\delta_{\bf g}
, and
\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+\beta_{1}
,
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
) one has in this case by definition (
2.3.4
) that
[\beta]=[\beta_{1}]+[\beta_{2}]+1\geq-1
(resp.
[\beta]=[\beta_{1}]+[\beta_{2}]+[\beta_{3}]+2\geq-1
, and
[\beta]=k+[\beta_{1}]\geq 0
,
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
). This implies
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}=0
if
[\beta]<-1
.

∎

Now using (
2.4.5
) and Lemma
2.4.1
we apply an inductive procedure to construct elements
\Phi_{\beta}
with two different choices of
\Phi_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
which yield the recentered maps and the canonical lift.

In the first case for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
we denote
\Phi_{\beta}=\Pi_{x\beta}
,
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}=\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
and for all purely polynomial multi–indices
\delta_{{\bf n}}
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
we define

(2.4.8)

\Pi_{x\delta_{{\bf n}}}(y):=(y-x)^{{\bf n}}{\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\in\mathbb{P}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}=\mathbb{P}_{|{{\bf n}}|}\otimes V_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}\subset\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}},~{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},

where
{\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\in W_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}^{*}\otimes V=V_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}
is the natural operator identifying
W_{\delta_{\bf n}}
and
V
.

Assuming that for a non–purely polynomial multi–index
\beta
elements
\Pi_{x\gamma}\in\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
for
|\gamma|<|\beta|
are given, and
\Pi_{x\gamma}=0
for
\gamma\not\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp}
with
|\gamma|<|\beta|
, we introduce
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}=\Phi_{\beta}^{-}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
by (
2.4.5
).

Then we define

(2.4.9)

\Pi_{x\beta}(y)=(\mathcal{K}\Pi_{x\beta}^{-})(y)-\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}:|{{\bf n}}|<|\beta|}\partial^{{\bf n}}(\mathcal{K}\Pi_{x\beta}^{-})(x)\frac{(y-x)^{{\bf n}}}{{{\bf n}}!},

where for
{{\bf n}}=(n_{0},n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})

{{\bf n}}!=\prod_{i=0}^{3}n_{i}!
and the sum in the last term is void if
|\beta|\leq 0
.

By Lemma
2.4.1
(ii) and (iii)
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}=0
if
\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, and
if
[\beta]<0
then
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}\in\mathbb{P}_{|\beta|-2}\otimes V_{\beta}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
. Therefore, since any polynomial of parabolic degree at most
n
is equal to its Taylor polynomial of parabolic degree
n
, and by Lemma
2.2.2
(i) the subspace
\mathbb{P}_{n}\otimes V_{\beta}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
is invariant under the action of the operator
\mathcal{K}
, definition (
2.4.9
) implies that
\Pi_{x\beta}=0
if
[\beta]<0
, i.e.
\Pi_{x\beta}
satisfies the same property as
\Pi_{x\gamma}
with
|\gamma|<|\beta|
in the induction assumption.

This assumption obviously holds for
\Pi_{x0}
as
[0]=0
, and
\alpha=|0|
is the minimal possible value of the homogeneity which is only achieved at
\beta=0
. Therefore the inductive procedure outlined above uniquely defines
\Pi_{x\beta}
and
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
for all
\beta
. Moreover,
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}=0
if
\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, and
\Pi_{x\beta}=0
for
\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp}
,

(2.4.10)

\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}=0~{\rm for}~\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime},

(2.4.11)

\Pi_{x\beta}=0~{\rm for}~[\beta]<0,~\beta\neq\delta_{{\bf n}},~{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}.

Also, by definition (
2.4.8
) and (
2.4.9
) one has

(2.4.12)

\partial^{\bf n}(\Pi_{x\beta})(y)=\partial^{\bf n}(\mathcal{K}\Pi_{x\beta}^{-})(y)~{\rm for~any}~x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{4},~\beta\in{\bf Mult},~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}

~{\rm such~that}~|{\bf n}|>|\beta|.

In the second case we denote
\Phi_{\beta}={\bf\Pi}_{\beta}
,
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}={\bf\Pi}_{\beta}^{-}
and for all purely polynomial multi–indices
\delta_{{\bf n}}
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
we define

(2.4.13)

{\bf\Pi}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}(y):=y^{{\bf n}}{\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\in\mathbb{P}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}=\mathbb{P}_{|{{\bf n}}|}\otimes V_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}\subset\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}},~{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}.

Assuming that for a non–purely polynomial multi–index
\beta
elements
{\bf\Pi}_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
for
|\gamma|<|\beta|
are given, and
{\bf\Pi}_{\gamma}=0
if
[\gamma]<-1
, we introduce
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}^{-}=\Phi_{\beta}^{-}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
by (
2.4.5
).

Then we define

(2.4.14)

{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}=\mathcal{K}{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}^{-}.

By Lemma
2.4.1
(i)
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}^{-}=0
if
\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
, and by Lemma
2.4.1
(iv)
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}^{-}=0
if
[\beta]<-1
, and hence by (
2.4.14
)
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}=0
if
[\beta]<-1
. Obviously for
{\bf\Pi}_{0}
one has
[0]=0\geq-1
, and
\alpha=|0|
is the minimal possible value of the homogeneity which is only achieved at
\beta=0
. Also, for
{\bf\Pi}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
we have
[\delta_{{\bf n}}]=-1
.

Therefore the inductive procedure described above uniquely defines
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}
and
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}^{-}
for all
\beta
. Moreover,
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}^{-}=0
if
[\beta]<-1
or
\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
, and
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}=0
if
[\beta]<-1
,

(2.4.15)

{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}^{-}={\bf\Pi}_{\beta}=0~{\rm for}~[\beta]<-1,~{\bf\Pi}_{\delta_{\bf n}}^{-}=0,~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}.

With the help of the elements
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}
and
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}^{-}
one can define recentered maps in the sense of Hairer as follows.
For any vector space
W
, denote by
{\rm Id}_{W}
its identity linear automorphism.
Let
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
, and define

(2.4.16)

\tau_{\beta}:={\rm Id}_{V_{\beta}^{*}}\in{\rm End}(V_{\beta}^{*})\simeq V_{\beta}^{*}\otimes V_{\beta},

and

(2.4.17)

\tau_{\beta}^{-}:={\rm Id}_{{V_{\beta}^{-}}^{*}}\in{\rm End}({V_{\beta}^{-}}^{*})\simeq{V_{\beta}^{-}}^{*}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}.

For any
\Phi_{\beta}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}
,
\Phi_{\beta}^{-}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
,
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
we define a linear map

\Phi:{\bf T}\to\mathcal{O},~{\bf T}:=\bigoplus_{\beta\in{\bf Mult}}V_{\beta}^{*}\oplus\bigoplus_{\beta\in{\bf Mult}}{V_{\beta}^{-}}^{*},

by

(2.4.18)

(\Phi\otimes{\rm Id}_{V_{\beta}})\tau_{\beta}=\Phi_{\beta},~(\Phi\otimes{\rm Id}_{V_{\beta}^{-}})\tau_{\beta}^{-}=\Phi_{\beta}^{-}.

We can apply this definition to
\Pi_{x\beta}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}
,
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
,
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
to get a linear map

(2.4.19)

\Pi_{x}:{\bf T}\to\mathcal{O}.

Due to properties (
2.4.10
) and (
2.4.11
)
\Pi_{x}|_{V_{\beta}^{*}}=0
for
\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp}
, and
\Pi_{x}|_{{V_{\beta}^{-}}^{*}}=0
for
\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. Therefore
\Pi_{x}
is completely determined by its restriction to
T\subset{\bf T}
,

(2.4.20)

\Pi_{x}:T\to\mathcal{O}.

The maps
\Pi_{x}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
are called recentered maps (we follow the terminology of
[
16
]
).

Similarly, applying definition (
2.4.18
) to
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}
and
{\bf\Pi}_{\beta}^{-}
,
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
we can define a linear map

(2.4.21)

{\bf\Pi}:{\bf T}\to\mathcal{O}.

Due to property (
2.4.15
)
{\bf\Pi}|_{V_{\beta}^{*}}=0
and
{\bf\Pi}|_{{V_{\beta}^{-}}^{*}}=0
for
[\beta]<-1
. Therefore
{\bf\Pi}
is completely determined by its restriction to

\bigoplus_{\beta\in{\bf Mult}:[\beta]\geq-1}V_{\beta}^{*}\oplus\bigoplus_{\beta\in{\bf Mult}:[\beta]\geq-1}{V_{\beta}^{-}}^{*}\subset{\bf T},

{\bf\Pi}:\bigoplus_{\beta\in{\bf Mult}:[\beta]\geq-1}V_{\beta}^{*}\oplus\bigoplus_{\beta\in{\bf Mult}:[\beta]\geq-1}{V_{\beta}^{-}}^{*}\to\mathcal{O}.

The map
{\bf\Pi}
is called the canonical lift.

In conclusion, we obtain certain local analytic bounds for
\Pi_{x\beta}
and
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
which are similar to those obtained in
[
27
]
, Proposition 8.27. These bounds are provisional in our approach. In fact, in Section
6.3
we shall derive global uniform estimates for
\Pi_{x\beta}
and
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
.

Proposition 2.4.2
.

Fix any norm on
V
, and for every
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
fix any norm on
W_{\beta}
. Denote by
|\cdot|
the induced operator norm on
V_{\beta}=V_{\beta}^{-}={\rm Hom}(W_{\beta},V)
. Then for every compact set
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, every
\rho>0
, and every
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
there exist constants
C_{{\mathfrak{K}},\beta}(\rho)
,
C_{{\mathfrak{K}},\beta}^{-}(\rho)\geq 0
such that

(2.4.22)

|\partial^{{\bf n}}(\Pi_{x\beta})(y)|\leq C_{{\mathfrak{K}},\beta}(\rho)|x-y|^{|\beta|-|{\bf n}|},~x,y\in{\mathfrak{K}},~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf n}|\leq|\beta|,

(2.4.23)

|\partial^{{\bf n}}(\Pi_{x\beta}^{-})(y)|\leq C_{{\mathfrak{K}},\beta}^{-}(\rho)|x-y|^{|\beta|-2-|{\bf n}|},~x,y\in{\mathfrak{K}},~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf n}|\leq|\beta|-2.

Proof.

For
\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
the bound in (
2.4.22
) follows from definition (
2.4.8
) and the bound in (
2.4.23
) is trivial by (
2.4.10
).

If
\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
and
|\beta|<0
the bound in (
2.4.22
) is obvious as
\Pi_{x\beta}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}=\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\beta}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\otimes V_{\beta}
, and if
\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
and
|\beta|-2<0
the bound in (
2.4.23
) is obvious as
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}=\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}
.

If
|\beta|\geq 0
the bound in (
2.4.22
) follows from the formula obtained by applying
\partial^{{\bf n}}
to (
2.4.9
) and from the expression for the remainder in the generalized Taylor formula (see
[
27
]
, Appendix A and the proof of Proposition 8.27 in
[
27
]
). If
|\beta|-2\geq 0
the bound in (
2.4.23
) follows from (
2.4.22
) and from the formula obtained by applying
\partial^{{\bf n}}
to (
2.4.5
) in the case of
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
.

∎

This proposition immediately implies the following corollary.

Corollary 2.4.3
.

(2.4.24)

\partial^{\bf n}(\Pi_{x\beta})(x)=0~{\rm for~any}~x\in\mathbb{R}^{4},~\beta\in{\bf Mult},~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}~{\rm such~that}~|{\bf n}|<|\beta|,

and

(2.4.25)

\partial^{\bf n}(\Pi_{x\beta}^{-})(x)=0~{\rm for~any}~x\in\mathbb{R}^{4},~\beta\in{\bf Mult},~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}

{\rm such~that}~|{\bf n}|<|\beta|-2.

2.5.
Automorphisms relating the recentered maps and the canonical lift

In this section, as a technical tool for defining structure group maps we introduce certain automorphisms relating the recentered maps and the canonical lift. For this purpose we consider a formal power series algebra in terms of which these automorphisms are defined. Firstly, we introduce the relevant notions and terminology.

Let
f_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V)
be a linear basis of
V={\mathfrak{k}}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
{\bf z}_{\bf g}
,
{{\bf z}}^{i}_{{\bf n}}
,
i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V)
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
formal variables. Consider the algebra

{\rm Sym}[[{{\bf z}}]]:={\rm Sym}[[{{\bf z}}_{{\bf g}},\{{{\bf z}}^{i}_{{\bf n}}:i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}\}]]

of formal formal power series in the variables
{\bf z}_{\bf g}
,
{\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}}
,
i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V)
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
with coefficients in the algebra
{\rm Sym}
.

For any monomial in
{\rm Sym}[[{\bf z}]]
in the variables
{\bf z}_{\bf g}
,
{\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}}
,
i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V)
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
we introduce its
{\bf Mult}
–valued multi–index degree
{\rm deg_{m}}
by requiring that
{\rm deg_{m}}({\bf z}_{\bf g})=\delta_{\bf g}
,
{\rm deg_{m}}({\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}})=\delta_{\bf n}
,
i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V)
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
and that the multi–index degree of the product of two monomials is equal to the sum of their multi–index degrees.

For any element
Z\in{\rm Sym}[[{{\bf z}}]]
and any multi–index
\beta
we denote by
(Z)_{\beta}
the sum of all monomials of multi–index degree
\beta
which appear in
Z
. This sum is always finite since the set of multi–indices is the free commutative monoid generated by the elements
\delta_{\bf g}
and
\delta_{{\bf n}}
,
{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, and hence every its element is represented as a sum of other elements in finitely many ways.

Identifying
V
with
W_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}
we define elements

{\bf z}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\rm dim}(V)}f_{i}{\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}}\in W_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}[\{{\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}}:i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V)\}]\subset{\rm Sym}[[{\bf z}]].

For any multi–index
\beta=l\delta_{\bf g}+\sum_{i=0}^{p}k_{i}\delta_{{{\bf n}}_{i}}
, let

{\bf z}^{\beta}={\bf z}_{\bf g}^{l}{\bf z}_{\delta_{{{\bf n}}_{1}}}^{k_{1}}\ldots{\bf z}_{\delta_{{{\bf n}}_{p}}}^{k_{p}}\in W_{\beta}[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}^{j}_{{{\bf n}}_{i}}:j=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),i=1,\ldots,p\}]\subset{\rm Sym}[[{\bf z}]],

where the product in
{\rm Sym}[[{\bf z}]]
is used in the right hand side.

By the definition the coefficients of the polynomial
{\bf z}^{\beta}
in the variables
{\bf z}_{\bf g}
and
{\bf z}^{j}_{{{\bf n}}_{i}}
,
j=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V)
,
i=1,\ldots,p
form a linear basis of
W_{\beta}
, and hence any
\Phi_{\beta}\in\mathcal{O}(V)\otimes W_{\beta}^{*}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\beta}
is completely determined by its values on these coefficients, i.e. by the polynomial

\Phi_{\beta}({\bf z}^{\beta})\in\mathcal{O}(V)[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}^{j}_{{{\bf n}}_{i}}:j=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),i=1,\ldots,p\}],

in
{\bf z}_{\bf g}
,
{\bf z}^{j}_{{{\bf n}}_{i}}
,
j=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V)
,
i=1,\ldots,p
the coefficients of which are obtained by evaluating
\Phi_{\beta}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}\simeq\mathcal{O}(V)\otimes W_{\beta}^{*}
on the coefficients of the polynomial
{\bf z}^{\beta}\in W_{\beta}[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}^{j}_{{{\bf n}}_{i}}:j=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),i=1,\ldots,p\}]
with the help of the inclusion

\mathcal{O}_{\beta}\otimes W_{\beta}\simeq\mathcal{O}(V)\otimes W_{\beta}^{*}\otimes W_{\beta}\subset\mathcal{O}\otimes{\rm Sym}^{*}\otimes{\rm Sym}

and of the natural map

(2.5.1)

\mathcal{O}\otimes{\rm Sym}^{*}\otimes{\rm Sym}\to\mathcal{O}

induced by pairing (
2.3.1
).

If
\Phi_{\beta}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}
are defined for all multi–indices
\beta
it is also convenient to introduce the formal power series

(2.5.2)

\Phi({\bf z}):=\sum_{\beta\in{\bf Mult}}\Phi_{\beta}({\bf z}^{\beta})\in\mathcal{O}(V)[[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}},i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}\}]],

and any element of
\mathcal{O}(V)[[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}},i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}\}]]
uniquely determines elements of
\mathcal{O}_{\beta}
for all multi–indices
\beta
.

Now for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
we define endomorphisms
F_{x}^{*}
of the algebra
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]:=\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}},i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}\}]]\subset{\rm Sym}[[{\bf z}]]
by requiring that

(2.5.3)

F_{x}^{*}1=1,~F_{x}^{*}{\bf z}_{\bf g}={\bf z}_{\bf g},

(2.5.4)

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}}^{V}\otimes F_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}:\\
|{\bf n}|\leq|{\bf m}|\end{array}}{{\bf m}\choose{\bf n}}(-x)^{{\bf m}-{\bf n}}({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{{\bf m}}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf m}}-

-\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:|{\bf n}|\leq|\beta|}\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}:\\
|{\bf m}|\leq|\beta|-|{\bf n}|\end{array}}\frac{(-x)^{\bf m}}{{\bf m}!}\partial^{{\bf n}+{\bf m}}(\mathcal{K}\Pi_{x\beta}^{-})(x)({\bf z}^{\beta}),

where

{{\bf m}\choose{\bf n}}=\frac{{\bf m}!}{{\bf n}!({\bf m}-{\bf n})!},

and as usual for
{\bf n}=(n_{0},n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
,
{\bf m}=(m_{0},m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})
we define
{{\bf m}\choose{\bf n}}=0
unless
m_{i}\geq n_{i}
for
i=0,1,2,3
. In the first line in (
2.5.4
) each
{\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf m}}
is regarded as an element of
W_{\delta_{{\bf m}}}\otimes\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]\supset W_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}[\{{\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}}:i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V)\}]
, so that (
2.5.4
) must hold in
V[[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}},i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}\}]]\simeq V\otimes\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
.

Since
V
is finite–dimensional this definition completely determines the action of
F_{x}^{*}
on the formal variables
{\bf z}_{\bf n}^{i}
and
{\bf z}_{\bf g}
. Below we shall see that the natural extension of
F_{x}^{*}
defined by (
2.5.3
), (
2.5.4
) to endomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
is well defined.

Definition (
2.5.3
), (
2.5.4
) is obtained by dualizing a similar definition of a dual map for regularity structures (see
[
27
]
, §8.2 or
[
16
]
, Section 2). The maps
F_{x}^{*}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
actually induce automorphisms of the model space
T
. We shall not need this fact. A similar property will be proved below for the structure group maps.

To confirm that
F_{x}^{*}
can be extended to automorphisms of the algebra
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
we firstly note that formula (
2.1.5
) for the homogeneity can be rewritten in the form

(2.5.5)

|\beta|=\alpha(1+[\beta])+\beta(g)+\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}})|{{\bf n}}|.

This implies that for
\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}
one has
|\beta|\in\alpha(\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\})+\mathbb{N}
. Since
\alpha\not\in\mathbb{Q}
, we deduce that for
\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}
one has
|\beta|\not\in\mathbb{Q}
,

(2.5.6)

\{|\beta|:\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\}\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}.

Therefore, if in the last sum in (
2.5.4
)
|\beta|=|{\bf n}|\in\mathbb{N}
then
[\beta]<0
. In this case by Lemma
2.4.1
(iii)
\Pi_{\beta}^{-}\in\mathbb{P}_{|\beta|-2}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
. This implies
\partial^{{\bf n}+{\bf m}}(\mathcal{K}\Pi_{x\beta}^{-})(x)=0
, and hence (
2.5.4
) takes the form

(2.5.7)

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}}^{V}\otimes F_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}:\\
|{\bf n}|\leq|{\bf m}|\end{array}}{{\bf m}\choose{\bf n}}(-x)^{{\bf m}-{\bf n}}({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{{\bf m}}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf m}}-

-\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}:|{\bf n}|<|\beta|}\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}:\\
|{\bf m}|<|\beta|-|{\bf n}|\end{array}}\frac{(-x)^{\bf m}}{{\bf m}!}\partial^{{\bf n}+{\bf m}}(\mathcal{K}\Pi_{x\beta}^{-})(x)({\bf z}^{\beta}),

as if
[\beta]\geq 0
one has
|\beta|\not\in\mathbb{Q}
by (
2.5.6
),
so
|\beta|\leq|{\bf n}|\in\mathbb{N}
implies
|\beta|<|{\bf n}|
, and
|{\bf m}|\leq|\beta|-|{\bf n}|
implies
|{\bf m}|<|\beta|-|{\bf n}|
.

Since the right hand sides of the second expression in (
2.5.3
) and of (
2.5.7
) do not contain zero powers of the formal variables
{\bf z}_{\bf g}
,
{\bf z}^{i}_{{\bf n}},i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
all
F_{x}^{*}
can be naturally extended by multiplicativity to continuous automorphisms of the algebra
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
.

Next, we study properties of the endomorphisms
F_{x}^{*}
. For this purpose we introduce some relevant notions and notation.
First note that any linear map
F^{*}:\mathbb{R}[{\bf z}]\to\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
, where
\mathbb{R}[{\bf z}]
