\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathbb{B}_{pq}^{s}}^{\prime}
is an equivalent quasi–norm on
\mathbb{B}_{pq}^{s}
. The last statement can be obtained by replacing the moduli of real numbers with the norm
\left\|~\cdot~\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}
in the arguments in the proof of Proposition A5 (1) in
[
7
]
and by using these arguments verbatim. A similar statement for the usual Besov spaces can also be found in
[
45
]
, Corollary 1.12.

We shall also need the so-called spectral gap inequality in the case when the underlying probability space is the white noise space modeled on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\otimes V
, where
V
is a Euclidean space, with the covariance induced by the scalar product on
V
, so that its Cameron–Martin space
{\rm CM}
is
L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\otimes V
with the natural induced scalar product. In fact, in our case
V={\mathfrak{k}}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
where the scalar product is induced by
-(~\cdot~,~\cdot~)
on
{\mathfrak{k}}
, and by the standard Euclidean scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
fixed in the Introduction. Denote also by
{\rm WN}
the corresponding space of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\otimes V
–valued random variables. Note that the white noise space variable
\xi
can be regarded as an element of
{\rm WN}
.

For a an
E
–valued random variable
f
of random argument
\xi
we denote by
\delta_{\upsilon}f
or simply by
\delta f
, if it does not cause a confusion, its directional derivative with respect to an element
\upsilon\in{\rm CM}
,

\delta_{\upsilon}f(\xi)=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}f(\xi+t\upsilon).

If
\delta
appears in a formula several times it is meant that this is the directional derivative with respect to the same element
\upsilon
in all occurrences.

The spectral gap inequality will be used in the form of equation (187) in
[
5
]
,

(3.1.7)

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\lesssim|\mathbb{E}(f)|+\sup_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\upsilon\in\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}(L_{2}):\\
\left\|\left\|\upsilon\right\|_{L_{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}}\leq 1\end{array}}\left\|\delta_{\upsilon}f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}},

where
\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}(L_{2}):=\{F\in{\rm WN}:\left\|\left\|F\right\|_{L_{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}}<\infty\}
,
1\leq q<p^{*}\leq 2\leq p
,
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{*}}=1
, and the constant in the inequality depends on
p
.
\delta_{\upsilon}f
in the right hand side of the spectral gap inequality makes sense if
f
belongs to the Malliavin–Sobolev class
{\mathbb{W}}_{p}^{1}
(see
[
3
]
, Chapter 5 for their definition). Indeed,
\delta_{\upsilon}f
should be viewed as the Malliavin derivative of
f
evaluated at
\upsilon
. Recall that the Malliavin derivative is well defined as a closed operator from
\mathbb{L}_{p}
to
\mathbb{L}_{p}(L_{2})=\{F\in{\rm WN}:\left\|\left\|F\right\|_{L_{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}<\infty\}
for
p\geq 2
, with the domain being the Malliavin–Sobolev space
{\mathbb{W}}_{p}^{1}
(see
[
3
]
, Chapter 5). Hence by the natural duality for
\mathbb{L}_{p}(L_{2})
–spaces, for
p\geq 2
,
\mathbb{E}(\delta_{\upsilon}f)
is finite for
\upsilon\in\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}(L_{2})
,
f\in{\mathbb{W}}_{p}^{1}
. Note that by the Hölder inequality
|\mathbb{E}(\delta_{\upsilon}f)|\leq\left\|\delta_{\upsilon}f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}
as
1\leq q
, so, generally speaking, if
q>1
the right hand side of the spectral gap inequality in form (
3.1.7
) is finite under a restriction stronger than just
f\in{\mathbb{W}}_{p}^{1}
.

In fact, the spectral gap inequality with
q=1
holds for bounded cylindrical functions provided that the underlying probability measure is centered Gaussian which is the case in the case of a white noise. Then it can be extended to the Malliavin–Sobolev classes
{\mathbb{W}}_{p}^{1}
(see
[
3
]
, Section 5.5 for the case
p=2
, and
[
32
]
, Lemma 3.1 for a generalization if
p>2
).

3.2.
Schauder estimates and reconstruction theorem for germs

Following the strategy of
[
5
,
35
]
for establishing stochastic estimates for models we shall use stochastic versions of Schauder estimates for the operator
\mathcal{K}
acting on germs which are similar to those presented in
[
6
]
in the deterministic case (see also
[
22
]
, Chapter 14). These estimates are closely related to multilevel Schauder estimates which initially appeared in
[
30
]
.

Another important ingredient used in
[
5
,
35
]
is a stochastic version of the reconstruction theorem for germs. We state and use it in a form similar to Theorem 3.2 in
[
7
]
. This theorem is, in turn, a generalization of the reconstruction theorem suggested in
[
30
]
for modelled distributions (see also
[
13
,
26
]
for other versions of the reconstruction theorem).

Note that a major difference between the Schauder estimates and the reconstruction theorem used in
[
5
]
and analogous statements in this section is that in the latter case the rescaling parameter, which naturally appears in both cases, takes values in a bounded interval (compare with the discussion in Section
1.2
of the scaling properties of the white noise and of less rough noises utilized in
[
5
]
).

Recall that a
p
–germ,
1\leq p\leq\infty
, is a family
F=(F_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}}
of
E
–valued random distributions
F_{x}
such that for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}
the map

x\mapsto\left\|F_{x}(\varphi)\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}

is measurable.

If the random distributions
F_{x}
are smooth, we call the germ
F
smooth.

For a
p
–germ
F
,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
,
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
,
a\leq c
,
\bar{\lambda}>0
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, any measurable subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}
we define

\left\|F\right\|_{pq\Omega\bar{\lambda}\varphi}^{b}:=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}}\frac{\left\|F_{x}(\varphi_{x}^{\lambda})\right\|_{pq\Omega x}}{\lambda^{b}},

\left\|F\right\|_{pq\Omega\bar{\lambda}\varphi}^{ac}:=\sup_{h\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:|h|\leq 2\bar{\lambda}}\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}}\frac{\left\|(F_{x+h}-F_{x})(\varphi_{x}^{\lambda})\right\|_{pq\Omega x}}{\lambda^{a}(\lambda+|h|)^{c-a}},

(3.2.1)

\left\|F_{x}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}r}^{b}:=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}}\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}}\frac{\left\|F_{x}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}}{\lambda^{b}},

(3.2.2)

\left\|F_{x}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}r}^{\circ b}:=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}}\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}_{b}}\frac{\left\|F_{x}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}}{\lambda^{b}},

(3.2.3)

\left\|F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}\Omega r}^{b}:=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}}\frac{\left\|\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}}\left\|F_{x}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\right\|_{L_{q}(\Omega,x)}}{\lambda^{b}},

(3.2.4)

\left\|F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}\Omega r}^{\circ b}:=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}}\frac{\left\|\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}_{b}}\left\|F_{x}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\right\|_{L_{q}(\Omega,x)}}{\lambda^{b}}.

We shall need the following pointwise and local Schauder type estimates for the action of the operator
\mathcal{K}
on germs in Besov type seminorms.

Proposition 3.2.1
.

For any
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
,
b\in\mathbb{R}
,
r>|b+2|
,
\bar{\lambda}>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
, any compact subset
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and any
p
–germ
F
one has

(3.2.5)

\left\|\mathcal{K}F_{x}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}r}^{\circ b+2}\lesssim\left\|F_{x}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}^{\prime}r}^{\circ b}\leq\left\|F_{x}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}^{\prime}r}^{b},~\bar{\lambda}^{\prime}=\max\{2\bar{\lambda},2\},

and

(3.2.6)

\left\|\mathcal{K}F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}{\mathfrak{K}}r}^{\circ b+2}\lesssim\left\|F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{K}}r}^{\circ b}\leq\left\|F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{K}}r}^{b},

where
(\mathcal{K}F_{x})(\varphi)=F_{x}(\mathcal{K}^{-}\varphi)
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{K}^{-}
is the integral operator with the kernel
K^{-}(x,y):=K(-x+y)
, the constants in the first inequalities in (
3.2.5
) and (
3.2.6
) only depend on
b
and
r
.

Note that the second inequalities in (
3.2.5
) and (
3.2.6
) are trivial, and the first ones can be obtained following the arguments in the proof of Theorem 14.17 in
[
22
]
verbatim with
\zeta=F_{x}
, where, in the discussion after the identity (14.7), instead of the modulus of real numbers one has to use the seminorm
\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}_{b+2}}\left\|F_{x}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}
in the case of (
3.2.5
) and the seminorm
\left\|\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}_{b+2}}\left\|F_{x}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\right\|_{L_{q}({\mathfrak{K}},x)}
in the case of (
3.2.6
). A similar result is also stated and proved as Theorem 5.4 in
[
6
]
.

In view of definition (
2.4.9
) we shall also need the following weakened stochastic version of Theorem 5.10 in
[
6
]
which allows to estimate seminorms (
3.2.2
) and (
3.2.4
) in terms of (
3.2.1
) and (
3.2.3
), respectively.

Proposition 3.2.2
.

Let
F
be a smooth
p
–germ,
1\leq p\leq\infty
. Then for any
1\leq q\leq\infty
,
b\in\mathbb{R}
,
b\geq 0
,
b\not\in\mathbb{N}
,
r>b
,
\bar{\lambda}>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
, any compact subset
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
one has

(3.2.7)

\left\|F^{b}_{x}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}r}^{b}\lesssim\left\|F_{x}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}r}^{\circ b},

and

(3.2.8)

\left\|F^{b}\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}{\mathfrak{K}}r}^{b}\lesssim\left\|F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}{\mathfrak{K}}r}^{\circ b},

where the constants in the inequalities in (
3.2.7
) and (
3.2.8
) only depend on
b
and
r
, and
F^{b}
is the smooth germ defined by

F^{b}_{x}(y):=F_{x}(y)-\sum_{{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}:|{\bf n}|<b}\partial^{\bf n}F_{x}(x)\frac{(y-x)^{\bf n}}{{\bf n}!}.

The proof of this proposition follows verbatim the arguments in the proof of Theorem 5.10 in
[
6
]
with the moduli of real numbers replaced by appropriate seminorms as described in the discussion after Proposition
3.2.1
.

From Propositions
3.2.1
and
3.2.2
we immediately obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 3.2.3
.

For any
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
,
b\not\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r>b+2\geq 0
,
\bar{\lambda}>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
, any compact subset
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and any smooth
p
–germ
F
one has

(3.2.9)

\left\|(\mathcal{K}F)_{x}^{b+2}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}r}^{b+2}\lesssim\left\|F_{x}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}^{\prime}r}^{b},~\bar{\lambda}^{\prime}=\max\{2\bar{\lambda},2\},

and

(3.2.10)

\left\|(\mathcal{K}F)^{b+2}\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}{\mathfrak{K}}r}^{b+2}\lesssim\left\|F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{K}}r}^{b},

where the constants in the inequalities in (
3.2.9
) and (
3.2.10
) only depend on
b
and
r
.

Now we turn to the reconstruction theorem for germs.
The following proposition is an obvious stochastic version of Theorem 3.2 in
[
7
]
.

Proposition 3.2.4
.

Let
F
be a
p
–germ,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
,
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
,
a\leq c
,
c\neq 0
,
\bar{\lambda}>0
. Assume that there exists a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\varphi(x)dx\neq 0
, and for any compact subset
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}

\left\|F\right\|_{pq{\mathfrak{K}}\bar{\lambda}\varphi}^{b}<\infty.

Suppose also that

(3.2.11)

\left\|F\right\|_{pq{\mathfrak{K}}\bar{\lambda}\varphi}^{ac}<\infty.

Then there exists an
E
–valued random distribution
\mathcal{R}(F)
such that for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
r>\max\{-a,-b\}
and any compact subset
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
one has

\left\|\mathcal{R}(F)-F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}{\mathfrak{K}}r}^{c}\lesssim\left\|F\right\|_{pq{\mathfrak{K}}_{2\bar{\lambda}}\bar{\lambda}\varphi}^{ac},

where
\mathcal{R}(F)
is regarded as a germ independent of the base point, i.e.
\mathcal{R}(F)_{x}=\mathcal{R}(F)
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
, the constant in the inequality is independent of
{\mathfrak{K}}
, and
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2\bar{\lambda}}:={\mathfrak{K}}+B_{2\bar{\lambda}}=\{x+y:x\in{\mathfrak{K}},y\in B_{2\bar{\lambda}}\}
.

\mathcal{R}(F)
is unique if
c>0
, and if
F_{x}(x)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
is a continuous
E
–valued random distribution then
\mathcal{R}(F)(x)=F_{x}(x)
.

The proof of this proposition can be obtained using the arguments in the proof of Theorem 3.2 in
[
7
]
verbatim with the moduli of real numbers replaced by appropriate
\mathbb{L}_{p}
–norms.

For a
p
–germ
F
,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
,
a,b,c,c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,
a\leq c
,
c^{\prime}\geq 0

\bar{\lambda}>0
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, any compact subset
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}
we define

\left\|F\right\|_{pqR\bar{\lambda}\varphi}^{acc^{\prime}}:=\sup_{h\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:|h|\leq 2\bar{\lambda}}\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}}\frac{\left\|(F_{x+h}-F_{x})(\varphi_{x+h}^{\lambda})\right\|_{pqB_{R}x}}{\lambda^{a}(\lambda+|h|)^{c-a}(\lambda+R+|h|)^{c^{\prime}}},

\left\|F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}Rr}^{bb^{\prime}}:=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}}\frac{\left\|\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}}\left\|F_{x}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\right\|_{L_{q}(B_{R},x)}}{\lambda^{b}(\lambda+R)^{b^{\prime}}}.

The next proposition is another version of Theorem 3.2 in
[
7
]
the proof of which is obtained by a minor modification of the proof of the reconstruction bound in
[
7
]
, Section 4.4, replacing the moduli of real numbers by appropriate
\mathbb{L}_{p}
–norms.

Proposition 3.2.5
.

Let
F
be a
p
–germ,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
,
a,b,c,c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,
a\leq c
,
c\neq 0
,
c^{\prime}\geq 0
,
\bar{\lambda}>0
. Let
F_{x}(x)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a continuous
E
–valued random distribution and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}
a function such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\varphi(x)dx\neq 0
.

(i) Assume that for some
R>0

(3.2.12)

\left\|F\right\|_{pqR^{\prime}\bar{\lambda}\varphi}^{acc^{\prime}}\leq C,0<R^{\prime}\leq R+\bar{\lambda}

for some
C>0
independent of
R^{\prime}
and
R
.

Then for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
r>-a
one has

\left\|\mathcal{R}(F)-F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}Rr}^{cc^{\prime}}\lesssim C,

where
\mathcal{R}(F)(x)=F_{x}(x)
is regarded as a germ independent of the base point, i.e.
\mathcal{R}(F)_{x}=\mathcal{R}(F)
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and the constant in the inequality is independent of
R
.

(ii) If
F_{x+h}(\varphi_{y+h}^{\lambda})=_{\rm law}F_{x}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{y})
,
x,y,h\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}
and

(3.2.13)

\left\|F\right\|_{pqR^{\prime}\bar{\lambda}\varphi}^{acc^{\prime}}\leq C^{\prime},0<R^{\prime}\leq R

for some
C^{\prime}>0
independent of
R^{\prime}
and
R
then for
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
r>-a
one has

\left\|\mathcal{R}(F)-F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}Rr}^{cc^{\prime}}\lesssim C^{\prime},

where the constant in the inequality is independent of
R

Conditions of type (
3.2.11
), (
3.2.12
), (
3.2.13
) are called coherence conditions.

From the previous proposition we immediately obtain the following obvious corollary.

Corollary 3.2.6
.

(i) Assume that the conditions in part (i) of the previous proposition are satisfied, and
\mathcal{R}(F)(x)=F_{x}(x)=0
. Then for
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
r>-a
one has

(3.2.14)

\left\|F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}Rr}^{cc^{\prime}}\lesssim C,

where
C
is defined in the previous proposition and the constant in the inequality is independent of
R
.

(ii) Assume that the conditions in part (ii) of the previous proposition are satisfied, and
\mathcal{R}(F)(x)=F_{x}(x)=0
. Then for
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
r>-a
one has

\left\|F\right\|_{pq\bar{\lambda}Rr}^{cc^{\prime}}\lesssim C^{\prime},

where
C^{\prime}
is defined in the previous proposition and the constant in the inequality is independent of
R
.

3.3.
Differentiation of the recentered maps

In this section we obtain some results related to Malliavin derivatives of the recentered maps required in the proof of stochastic estimates for them. We refer the reader to
[
5
]
, Section 2.5 for motivations of the constructions in this section.

First note that by the definition the random variables
\Pi_{x\beta}
and
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
depend polynomially on the white noise space variable
\xi
via the mollification
\xi^{\rho}(x)=\xi(\eta_{x}^{\rho})
. Definition (
2.4.4
) and induction over
|\beta|
using formulas (
2.4.5
), (
2.4.9
) show that
\Pi_{x\beta}
and
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
have directional derivatives of polynomial growth along all directions in the Cameron–Martin space (see the discussion in
[
25
]
, Section 2.2.2. for more details). Therefore
\Pi_{x\beta}
and
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
are Malliavin differentiable, and the spectral gap inequality is satisfied for them (see Lemma 2.23 in
[
25
]
). We shall frequently use this fact without comments.

Moreover,
\delta_{\upsilon}\Pi_{x\beta}
and
\delta_{\upsilon}\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
are well defined for random
\upsilon
with values in
{\rm CM}
. We shall use the symbols
\delta_{\upsilon}\Pi_{x\beta}
and
\delta_{\upsilon}\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
in this sense without comments.
In fact, since the random variables
\Pi_{x\beta}
and
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
depend polynomially on the white noise space variable
\xi
via the mollification
\xi^{\rho}(x)
,
\delta_{\upsilon}\Pi_{x\beta}
and
\delta_{\upsilon}\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
take values in
\mathcal{O}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}
, respectively, for all random
\upsilon
with values in
{\rm CM}
.

Note also that by (
2.4.9
)

(\delta\Pi_{x\beta})(y)=(\mathcal{K}\delta\Pi_{x\beta}^{-})(y)-\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}:|{{\bf n}}|<|\beta|}\partial^{{\bf n}}(\mathcal{K}\delta\Pi_{x\beta}^{-})(x)\frac{(y-x)^{{\bf n}}}{{{\bf n}}!}.

Below for linear endomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
and induced endomorphisms of
{\rm Sym}[[{\bf z}]]
we use the notation and conventions of Section
2.5
.

We proceed with the following algebraic statement which will be used for the study of the properties of the Malliavin derivative of
\Pi_{0\beta}
.

Proposition 3.3.1
.

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
f_{\bf n}\in V\otimes\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
,
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
, where
{\varepsilon}
is as in the definition of
\alpha=-\frac{1}{2}-{\varepsilon}
, there exists a unique continuous linear endomorphism
F^{*}
of
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
such that

(3.3.1)

F^{*}1=0,~F^{*}{\bf z}_{\bf g}=0,

(3.3.2)

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\otimes F^{*}){\bf z}_{\bf n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&{\rm if}~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf n}|\geq 2-{\varepsilon}\\
f_{\bf n}&{\rm if}~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}\end{array}\right.,

(3.3.3)

F^{*}(ZZ^{\prime})=F^{*}(Z)\Gamma_{x0}^{*}(Z^{\prime})+\Gamma_{x0}^{*}(Z)F^{*}(Z^{\prime})

for any
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
.

F^{*}
satisfies

(3.3.4)

(F)_{0}^{\gamma}=0~{\rm if}~\gamma\neq\delta_{\bf n}~{\rm for~some}~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon},

and

(3.3.5)

(F)_{\beta}^{\gamma}=0~{\rm if}~|\gamma|\geq|\beta|+\frac{d}{2}.

We start the proof of this proposition with the following lemma which is similar to Lemma
2.6.2
.

Lemma 3.3.2
.

For any linear map
F^{*}:\mathbb{R}[{\bf z}]\to\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
which satisfies (
3.3.1
) and (
3.3.3
) with arbitrary
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}[{\bf z}]
and any multi–index
\gamma=l\delta_{\bf g}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{i}}\neq 0
,
{\bf m}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
one has

(3.3.6)

(F)_{\beta}^{\gamma}=\hskip 284.52756pt

=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{p}\in{\bf Mult}:\\
\beta_{1}+\ldots+\beta_{p}+l\delta_{\bf g}=\beta\end{array}}\hskip-14.22636pt(\Gamma_{x0})_{\beta_{1}}^{\delta_{{\bf m}_{1}}}\ldots(\Gamma_{x0})_{\beta_{k-1}}^{\delta_{{\bf m}_{k-1}}}(F)_{\beta_{k}}^{\delta_{{\bf m}_{k}}}(\Gamma_{x0})_{\beta_{k+1}}^{\delta_{{\bf m}_{k+1}}}\ldots(\Gamma_{x0})_{\beta_{p}}^{\delta_{{\bf m}_{p}}},

where we assume that an empty sum is zero and an empty product is 1.

Moreover, if
p>1
or
l>0
then in the non–zero terms in the sum in (
3.3.6
)
|\beta_{i}|<|\beta|
,
i=1,\ldots,p
.

Proof.

Firstly, similarly to the proof of (
2.6.16
), relation (
3.3.6
) follows from (
3.3.1
) and (
3.3.3
).

From definition (
2.1.5
) it follows that
|\cdot|-\alpha
is a linear function on
{\bf Mult}
achieving its minimum equal to zero at
\beta=0
, and hence for each non–zero term in (
3.3.6
) we have as in (
2.6.17
)

(3.3.7)

|\beta|-\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{p}(|\beta_{i}|-\alpha)+l(\alpha+1),~|\beta_{i}|-\alpha\geq 0,~i=1,\ldots,p.

Since
(\Gamma_{x0})_{0}^{\delta_{{\bf m}_{i}}}=0
,
i=1,\ldots,p
by (
2.6.2
) as
|\delta_{{\bf m}_{i}}|=|{\bf m}_{i}|\geq 0>|0|=\alpha
, we may assume that for each fixed
k
and for any
i\neq k
in the right hand side of (
3.3.6
) one has
\beta_{i}\neq 0
, and hence by Lemma
2.3.1

|\beta_{i}|-\alpha>0
. Note also that
l(\alpha+1)\geq 0
.
Therefore, if we assume that
p>1
or
l>0
, there is at least one strictly positive term in the right hand side of (
3.3.7
) which implies
|\beta_{i}|-\alpha<|\beta|-\alpha
,
i=1,\ldots,p
, or
|\beta_{i}|<|\beta|
,
i=1,\ldots,p
.

∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3.1
.
Firstly, conditions (
3.3.1
), (
3.3.2
) and formula (
3.3.6
) uniquely define a linear map
F^{*}:\mathbb{R}[{\bf z}]\to\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
which satisfies (
3.3.3
) with arbitrary
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}[{\bf z}]
. Using Lemma
2.5.1
(i) we show that this map can be extended to a continuous linear map
F^{*}:\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]\to\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
, so that (
3.3.3
) will hold with arbitrary
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
by continuity. In order to verify condition (
2.5.15
) it suffices to show that
F^{*}:\mathbb{R}[{\bf z}]\to\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
satisfies (
3.3.4
) and (
3.3.5
) which will also immediately imply that the same properties hold for the extension
F^{*}:\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]\to\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
.

We start with (
3.3.4
). If
\gamma=0
then
(F)_{0}^{0}=(F^{*}1)_{0}=0
by (
3.3.1
).

If
\gamma\neq 0
then the right hand side of (
3.3.6
) with
\beta=0
does not vanish only if
l=0
and
\beta_{i}=0
,
i=1,\ldots,p
. But in this case
(\Gamma_{x0})_{0}^{\delta_{{\bf m}_{i}}}=0
,
i=1,\ldots,p
by (
2.6.2
) as
|\delta_{{\bf m}_{i}}|=|{\bf m}_{i}|\geq 0>|0|=\alpha
. Thus the right hand side of (
3.3.6
) with
\beta=0
does not vanish only if
l=0
and
p=1
, i.e. if
\gamma=\delta_{\bf n}
for some
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, and by (
3.3.2
) in this case
|{\bf n}|<2+{\varepsilon}
.

Now we prove (
3.3.5
).
If
\gamma=0
property (
3.3.5
) is obvious as by (
3.3.1
)

(F)_{\beta}^{0}=(F^{*}1)_{\beta}=0
for all
\beta
.

If
\gamma=\delta_{\bf n}
for some
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, then by (
3.3.2
)
(F)_{\beta}^{\gamma}=(F)_{\beta}^{\delta_{\bf n}}
may be non–zero only if
|{\bf n}|<2+{\varepsilon}
, and in this case for any
\beta

|\gamma|=|\delta_{\bf n}|=|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}=\alpha+\frac{d}{2}\leq|\beta|+\frac{d}{2},

where at the last step we used Lemma
2.3.1
. This proves (
3.3.5
) for
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
.

Now for each fixed
\gamma\neq 0
,
\gamma\not\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
we prove (
3.3.5
) by induction over
|\beta|
.

If
|\beta|=\alpha
is minimal possible then
\beta=0
by Lemma
2.3.1
and (
3.3.5
) follows from (
3.3.4
) as for
\gamma=\delta_{\bf n}
,
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
one has

|\beta|+\frac{d}{2}=\alpha+\frac{d}{2}=2-{\varepsilon}>|{\bf n}|.

Now assume that (
3.3.5
) holds for all
\beta^{\prime}\in{\bf Mult}
with
|\beta^{\prime}|<|\beta|
,
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
. Note that in the considered case
\gamma\not\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
and
\gamma\neq 0
. Therefore
\gamma=l\delta_{\bf g}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{i}}\neq 0
,
{\bf m}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
, where
p>1
or
l>0
. Thus by Lemma
3.3.2
in the right hand side of (
3.3.6
)
|\beta_{i}|<|\beta|
,
i=1,\ldots,p
, and hence by (
2.6.2
), by the induction assumption and by (
3.3.7
)

|\beta|-\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{p}(|\beta_{i}|-\alpha)+l(\alpha+1)>\sum_{i=1}^{p}(|\delta_{{\bf m}_{i}}|-\alpha)+|l\delta_{\bf g}|-\alpha-\frac{d}{2}=|\gamma|-\frac{d}{2}-\alpha,

where at the last step we used linearity of
|\cdot|-\alpha
. Thus if
(F)^{\gamma}_{\beta}\neq 0
then
|\beta|>|\gamma|-\frac{d}{2}
.
This establishes the induction step and completes the proof.

∎

Now we state the main result of this section.

Proposition 3.3.3
.

For any random variable
\upsilon
with values in
{\rm CM}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
there exists a unique continuous linear endomorphism
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
of
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
which satisfies (
3.3.1
), (
3.3.3
), (
3.3.4
), (
3.3.5
), and

(3.3.8)

{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}{\bf z}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}=0,~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf n}|\geq 2-{\varepsilon},

(3.3.9)

(\partial^{\bf n}\delta\Pi_{0})(x)({\bf z})-({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{x}(x))({\bf z})=0~{\rm for}~|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon},

where
\delta=\delta_{\upsilon}
,
{\varepsilon}
is as in the definition of
\alpha=-\frac{1}{2}-{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. First observe that for any
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
by (
2.4.24
) one has

(3.3.10)

\partial^{\bf n}(\Pi_{x\beta})(x)=0~{\rm for~any}~x\in\mathbb{R}^{4},~\beta\in{\bf Mult},~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}~{\rm such~that}~|{\bf n}|<|\beta|,

and by (
2.5.6
) for
\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp}
and
|\beta|=|{\bf n}|
one has
\beta=\delta_{\bf m}
for some
{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
. Therefore using (
2.4.11
) and definition (
2.4.8
) we obtain in this case

(3.3.11)

\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{x\beta}(y)=\delta_{\beta}^{\delta_{\bf n}}{\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V},

where for
\beta,\gamma\in{\bf Mult}
we define

\delta_{\beta}^{\gamma}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&{\rm if}~\beta=\gamma\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right..

We can rewrite (
3.3.10
) and (
3.3.11
) as a single identity,

(3.3.12)

(\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{x}(x)({\bf z})-({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf n}})_{\beta}=0

for any
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
,
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
such that
|{\bf n}|\leq|\beta|
.

Note that by (
4.1.6
)
|{\bf n}|>|\beta|
implies
\beta({\bf m})=0
for
{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
|{\bf m}|\geq|{\bf n}|
. Therefore from (
3.3.12
) we immediately obtain

(3.3.13)

Y_{\bf n}(x)({\bf z}):=\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{x}(x)({\bf z})-({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf n}}\in

\in V[[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}_{\bf m}^{i}:i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},|{\bf m}|<|{\bf n}|\}]].

Now we define
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
using this property, Proposition
3.3.1
and induction over
|{\bf n}|
as follows.

First observe that if
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
and
|{\bf n}|=0
then
{\bf n}=0
. Thus (
3.3.13
) reduces to

Y_{\bf 0}(x)({\bf z})=\Pi_{x}(x)({\bf z})-({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf 0}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf 0}}\in V[[{\bf z}_{\bf g}]],

and we can define a unique continuous endomorphism
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
of
V[[{\bf z}_{\bf g}]]
using (
3.3.1
). In fact, this way we obtain the zero endomorphism. Therefore by Proposition
3.3.1
we can also define a unique continuous endomorphism of
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
, which we denote by the same symbol
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
, by requiring that

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf 0}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf 0}}=\delta\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z})-({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}Y_{\bf 0}(x))({\bf z})=\delta\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z}),

and

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf 0}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}=0,~{\bf n}\neq{\bf 0}.

{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
also satisfies (
3.3.1
), (
3.3.3
), (
3.3.4
) and (
3.3.5
).

Now assume that for
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
a continuous endomorphism
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
of
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
is defined and satisfies (
3.3.1
), (
3.3.3
), (
3.3.4
), (
3.3.5
),

(3.3.14)

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf m}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf m}}=

=\frac{1}{{\bf m}!}(\partial^{\bf m}\delta\Pi_{0})(x)({\bf z})-({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}Y_{\bf m}(x))({\bf z}),~{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf m}|<|{\bf n}|,

and

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf m}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{{\bf m}}}=0,~{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf m}|\geq|{\bf n}|.

Then by Proposition
3.3.1
we can define another unique continuous endomorphism of
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
, which we denote by the same symbol
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
, by requiring that

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf m}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf m}}~{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf m}|<|{\bf n}|

coincide with the values of the endomorphism
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
defined at the previous step of the induction,

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf n}}=\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}(\partial^{\bf n}\delta\Pi_{0})(x)({\bf z})-({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}Y_{\bf n}(x))({\bf z}),

where in the right hand side in the expression
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}Y_{\bf n}(x))({\bf z})
we use the action of
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
on
\mathbb{R}[[z]]
defined at the previous step of the induction, and

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf m}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{{\bf m}}}=0,~{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf m}|>|{\bf n}|.

By construction the action of the newly defined
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
introduced this way on
V[[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}_{\bf m}^{i}:i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},|{\bf m}|<|{\bf n}|\}]]
coincides with the action of
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
introduced at the previous step of the induction, and hence the newly defined
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
still satisfies (
3.3.14
) as by (
3.3.13
)

Y_{\bf n}(x)({\bf z})\in V[[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}_{\bf m}^{i}:i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},|{\bf m}|<|{\bf n}|\}]]
.
Thus by construction for the newly defined
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
we have

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf m}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf m}}=

=\frac{1}{{\bf m}!}(\partial^{\bf m}\delta\Pi_{0})(x)({\bf z})-({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}Y_{\bf m}(x))({\bf z}),~{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf m}|\leq|{\bf n}|,

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf m}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{{\bf m}}}=0,~{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf m}|>|{\bf n}|,

By Proposition
3.3.1
the newly defined
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
also satisfies (
3.3.1
), (
3.3.3
), (
3.3.4
) and (
3.3.5
).

Then we can proceed by induction until we reach
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
with the largest possible
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
. By construction the continuous endomorphism
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
of
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
defined at the last step satisfies (
3.3.1
), (
3.3.3
), (
3.3.4
), (
3.3.5
),

(3.3.15)

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf n}}=

=\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}(\partial^{\bf n}\delta\Pi_{0})(x)({\bf z})-({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}Y_{\bf n}(x))({\bf z}),~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon},

and

({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf m}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{{\bf n}}}=0,~{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},~|{\bf n}|\geq 2-{\varepsilon}.

Therefore for
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
one has from (
3.3.15
)

\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}(\partial^{\bf n}\delta\Pi_{0})(x)({\bf z})=({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf n}}+({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}Y_{\bf n}(x))({\bf z})=\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{x}(x))({\bf z}),

where at the last step we used (
3.3.13
). This is exactly identity (
3.3.9
). Note that by (
3.3.13
) properties (
3.3.9
) and (
3.3.15
) are equivalent.

Uniqueness of
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
follows from Proposition
3.3.1
and the inductive definition of
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
. This completes the proof.

∎

We shall also need the following analogue of property (
3.3.9
) for
\Pi_{0}^{-}(x)({\bf z})
and
\Pi_{x}^{-}(x)({\bf z})
.

Proposition 3.3.4
.

For any random variable
\upsilon
with values in
{\rm CM}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
the endomorphism
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
satisfies

(3.3.16)

\delta\Pi_{0}^{-}(x)({\bf z})-({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\Pi_{x}^{-}(x))({\bf z})-\upsilon^{\rho}(x)=0,

where
\delta=\delta_{\upsilon}
, and
\upsilon^{\rho}(x)=\upsilon(\eta_{x}^{\rho})
.

Proof.

First observe that (
2.4.4
) and (
2.4.5
) for
\Pi_{x\beta}
,
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}
can be rewritten as a single identity,

(3.3.17)

\Pi_{x}^{-}(y)({\bf z})={\bf z}_{\bf g}\mathcal{A}(\Pi_{x}(y)({\bf z}),\Pi_{x}(y)({\bf z}))+

+{\bf z}_{\bf g}^{2}\mathcal{B}(\Pi_{x}(y)({\bf z}),\Pi_{x}(y)({\bf z}),\Pi_{x}(y)({\bf z}))+c({\bf z})\Pi_{x}(y)({\bf z})+\xi^{\rho}(y),

where

c({\bf z}):=\sum_{k=1}^{4}c_{k}{\bf z}_{\bf g}^{k}.

Applying
\delta
to (
3.3.17
) with
x=0
and
y=x
and using (
3.3.9
) with
{\bf n}={\bf 0}
we obtain

(3.3.18)

\delta\Pi_{0}^{-}(x)({\bf z})={\bf z}_{\bf g}\mathcal{A}(({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\Pi_{x}(x))({\bf z}),\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z}))+

+{\bf z}_{\bf g}\mathcal{A}(\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z}),({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\Pi_{x}(x))({\bf z}))+{\bf z}_{\bf g}^{2}\mathcal{B}(({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\Pi_{x}(x))({\bf z}),\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z}),\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z}))+

+{\bf z}_{\bf g}^{2}\mathcal{B}(\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z}),({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\Pi_{x}(x))({\bf z}),\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z}))+{\bf z}_{\bf g}^{2}\mathcal{B}(\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z}),\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z}),({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\Pi_{x}(x))({\bf z}))+

+c({\bf z})({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\Pi_{x}(x))({\bf z})+\upsilon^{\rho}(x).

Using the fact that by (
2.6.6
)
\Pi_{0}(x)({\bf z})=(\Gamma_{x0}^{*}\Pi_{x}(x))({\bf z})
and properties (
2.6.7
), (
3.3.1
), (
3.3.3
) we can reduce the right hand side of (
3.3.18
) as follows

\delta\Pi_{0}^{-}(x)({\bf z})={\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\large\left({\bf z}_{\bf g}\mathcal{A}(\Pi_{x}(x)({\bf z}),\Pi_{x}(x)({\bf z}))+\right.

\left.+{\bf z}_{\bf g}^{2}\mathcal{B}(\Pi_{x}(x)({\bf z}),\Pi_{x}(x)({\bf z}),\Pi_{x}(x)({\bf z}))+c({\bf z})\Pi_{x}(x)({\bf z})\large\right)+\upsilon^{\rho}(x)=

=({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}\Pi_{x}^{-}(x))({\bf z})+\upsilon^{\rho},

where at the last step we used (
3.3.17
) with
y=x
and (
3.3.1
). This completes the proof.

∎

Now we study some algebraic properties of the endomorphism
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
.

Proposition 3.3.5
.

For any random variable
\upsilon
with values in
{\rm CM}
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
the endomorphism
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
satisfies the following properties:

(3.3.19)

({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\gamma}=0~{\rm for~any}~\gamma\in\mathcal{M},~\beta\in{\bf Mult},~[\beta]<0,

(3.3.20)

({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\gamma}=0~{\rm for~any}~\beta,\gamma\in{\bf Mult},~|\gamma|_{\prec}\geq|\beta|_{\prec}.

Proof.

We start with (
3.3.19
) which is obvious for
\gamma=0
by the first identity in (
3.3.1
) and by the definition of
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\gamma}=({\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}{\bf z}^{\gamma})_{\beta}
.

For any
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}
and
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
with minimal possible
|\beta|=\alpha
we have
\beta=0
, and the claim is void as
[0]=0
.

Now assume that for some
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
the statement is true for all
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}
and
\beta^{\prime}\in{\bf Mult}
with
|\beta^{\prime}|<|\beta|
.

For
\gamma=l\delta_{\bf g}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{i}}\neq 0
and
\gamma\not\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
, i.e.
l>0
or
p>1
, we proceed by induction over
|\beta|
the base of which is already established.

We use (
3.3.6
) with
F={\rm d}\Gamma_{x}
, where by Lemma
3.3.2

|\beta_{i}|<|\beta|
,
i=1,\ldots,p
as
l>0
or
p>1
. Thus by the induction assumption the k-th term in the sum over k in (
3.3.6
) vanishes unless
[\beta_{k}]\geq 0
in all summands which this term contains. By (
2.6.11
) this term also vanishes unless
\beta_{i}\in\mathcal{M}
,
i=1,\ldots,k-1,k+1,\ldots,p
in all summands which this term contains, and for all such
\beta_{i}
one, in particular, has
[\beta_{i}]\geq-1
. Thus, by the additivity of
[~\cdot~]
, in this case
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\gamma}=0
unless
[\beta]\geq l-p+1
.

But by the definition of
\gamma
one also has in this case
[\gamma]=l-p\geq-1
as
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}
. Thus
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\gamma}=0
unless
[\beta]\geq[\gamma]+1\geq 0
. This establishes the induction step if
\gamma\neq 0
and
\gamma\not\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
.

The case
\gamma=\delta_{\bf n}\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
,
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
is trivial if
|{\bf n}|\geq 2-{\varepsilon}
by (
3.3.8
), and for
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
we use (
3.3.9
) and rewrite it in terms of components with the help of (
2.4.11
) as follows

\partial^{\bf n}\delta\Pi_{0\beta}(x)=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp}}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{x\mu}(x)\circ({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\mu},

or, since

\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{x\delta_{\bf m}}(x)=(\partial^{\bf n}(y-x)^{\bf m})(x){\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf m}}}^{V}={\bf n}!\delta_{\delta_{\bf m}}^{\delta_{\bf n}}{\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V},

as

(3.3.21)

{\bf n}!{\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\circ({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\delta_{\bf n}}=\partial^{\bf n}\delta\Pi_{0\beta}(x)-\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{x\mu}(x)\circ({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\mu}.

If
[\beta]<0
then the first term in the right hand side of this formula vanishes as by (
2.4.11
)
\Pi_{0\beta}
is non–zero in this case only if
\beta=\delta_{\bf m}
for some
{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, and by (
2.4.8
) in this case
\Pi_{0\delta_{\bf m}}=x^{\bf m}
is independent of the white noise space variables, so
\delta\Pi_{0\beta}(x)=0
.

Also, for
[\beta]<0

({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\mu}=0
in the last sum in the right hand side of (
3.3.21
) for all
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}
by the part of the induction step already proved as
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
and
\mu\not\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
.

We deduce that if
[\beta]<0
then the right hand side of (
3.3.21
) vanishes, i.e.
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\delta_{\bf n}}=0
. This completes the proof of the induction step and of property (
3.3.19
).

Now we turn to (
3.3.20
) which trivially holds for
\gamma=0
and
\gamma=\delta_{\bf g}
by (
3.3.1
).

If
\gamma=\delta_{\bf n}
,
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
then
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}{\bf z}_{\delta_{n}}=0
for
|\delta_{n}|_{\prec}=|\delta_{n}|=|{\bf n}|\geq 2-{\varepsilon}
by (
3.3.8
). Thus
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\delta_{\bf n}}=0
for any
\beta
in this case.

If
|\delta_{n}|_{\prec}=|\delta_{n}|=|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
, by (
3.3.19
)
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\delta_{\bf n}}=0
for any
\beta
with
[\beta]<0
, and if
[\beta]\geq 0
we have by (
2.5.13
)

|\beta|_{\prec}=|\beta|+\frac{d}{2}([\beta]+1)\geq\alpha+\frac{d}{2}=2-{\varepsilon},

so that
|\beta|_{\prec}\geq 2-{\varepsilon}>|\delta_{n}|_{\prec}
for possible non–zero
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\delta_{\bf n}}
. This proves (
3.3.20
) for
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
.

If
\gamma=l\delta_{\bf g}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{i}}\neq 0
,
{\bf m}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
with
p>1
or
l>1
we proceed using (
3.3.6
) for
F={\rm d}\Gamma_{x}
. Assume that
|\gamma|_{\prec}\geq|\beta|_{\prec}
in (
3.3.6
) with
F={\rm d}\Gamma_{x}
. By the linearity of the function
|\cdot|_{+}:=|\cdot|_{\prec}-\frac{d}{2}-\alpha
this implies that for the k-th term in the sum in the right hand side of (
3.3.6
) and for all summands which this term contains one has

|l\delta_{\bf g}|_{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}|\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{i}}|_{+}=|\gamma|_{+}\geq|\beta|_{+}=|l\delta_{\bf g}|_{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}|\beta_{i}|_{+},

or

(3.3.22)

\sum_{i=1}^{p}|\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{i}}|_{\prec}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{p}|\beta_{i}|_{\prec},

where by (
2.6.4
) non–zero contributions to the right hand side of (
3.3.6
) may only occur when
|\beta_{i}|_{\prec}\geq|\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{i}}|_{\prec}
,
i=1,\ldots,k-1,k+1,\ldots,p
. This implies

\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}i=1\\
i\neq k\end{array}}^{p}|\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{i}}|_{\prec}\leq\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}i=1\\
i\neq k\end{array}}^{p}|\beta_{i}|_{\prec}

which by (
3.3.22
) yields
|\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{k}}|_{\prec}\geq|\beta_{k}|_{\prec}
.

Since we already proved (
3.3.20
) for
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
this forces
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta_{k}}^{\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{k}}}=0
. Thus the right hand side of (
3.3.6
) for
F={\rm d}\Gamma_{x}
vanishes in this case. This completes the proof of (
3.3.20
).

∎

From (
2.6.4
), (
2.6.11
), (
3.3.19
) and (
3.3.20
) we immediately deduce the following corollary.

Corollary 3.3.6
.

Let
S_{xy}:={\rm d}\Gamma_{x}-\Gamma_{xy}{\rm d}\Gamma_{y}
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Then

(3.3.23)

(S_{xy})_{\beta}^{\gamma}=0~{\rm for~any}~\gamma\in\mathcal{M},~\beta\in{\bf Mult},~[\beta]<0,

and

(3.3.24)

(S_{xy})_{\beta}^{\gamma}=0~{\rm for~any}~\beta,\gamma\in{\bf Mult},~|\gamma|_{\prec}\geq|\beta|_{\prec}.

In conclusion we state some other algebraic properties of
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}
and
S_{xy}
which will be used in the proof of the stochastic estimates for the model.

Lemma 3.3.7
.

Let
\gamma=l\delta_{\bf g}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\delta_{{{\bf m}}_{i}}
,
{\bf m}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
be a multi–index with
[\gamma]\geq-1
, and
p>1
or
l>0
. Then the following statements are true.

(i) Let
F
be an algebra endomorphism of
\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
which satisfies (
2.5.20
). Assume also that

(3.3.25)

\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp},~\beta\not\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp}\Longrightarrow(F)_{\beta}^{\gamma}=0,

Then in the non–zero terms in the right hand side of (
2.6.16
) one has
[\beta_{i}]\leq[\beta]
and
|\beta_{i}|_{\prec}<|\beta|_{\prec}
,
i=1,\ldots,p
.

(ii) Let
F^{*}:\mathbb{R}[{\bf z}]\to\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
be a linear map which satisfies (
3.3.1
) and (
3.3.3
) with arbitrary
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}[{\bf z}]
and assume that for any
m\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
one has
(F)_{\beta}^{\delta_{{\bf m}}}=0
if
[\beta]<-1
. Then in the non–zero terms in the right hand side of (
3.3.6
) one has
[\beta_{i}]\leq[\beta]
and
|\beta_{i}|_{\prec}<|\beta|_{\prec}
,
i=1,\ldots,p
.

(iii)
F={\rm d}\Gamma_{x}
and
F=S_{xy}={\rm d}\Gamma_{x}-\Gamma_{xy}{\rm d}\Gamma_{y}
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
satisfy the conditions of part (ii), and hence the statement in part (ii) holds for them.

Proof.

We prove (i). Part (ii) is established using the same arguments verbatim.

First observe that by (
3.3.25
) we may assume that
[\beta_{i}]\geq-1
,
i=1,\ldots,p
in all terms in the sum in the right hand side of (
2.6.16
). Therefore for every such term and for any
k=1,\ldots,p
by the definition of
[~\cdot~]
we have

[\beta]=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}i=1\\
i\neq k\end{array}}^{p}([\beta_{i}]+1)+[\beta_{k}]+l-p+1=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}i=1\\
i\neq k\end{array}}^{p}([\beta_{i}]+1)+[\beta_{k}]+[\gamma]+1\geq[\beta_{k}]

as
[\beta_{i}]+1\geq 0
,
i=1,\ldots,p
and
[\gamma]+1\geq 0
.

Now by the definition of the modified homogeneity and by Lemma
2.6.2
we deduce

|\beta|_{\prec}=|\beta|+\frac{d}{2}([\beta]+1)>|\beta_{k}|+\frac{d}{2}([\beta_{k}]+1)=|\beta_{k}|_{\prec}.

(iii) Note that both
F^{*}
in part (iii) satisfy (
3.3.1
) and (
3.3.3
) with arbitrary
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}[{\bf z}]
. For
F^{*}={\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
this is clear from the definition, and for
F^{*}={\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}-{\rm d}\Gamma_{y}^{*}\Gamma_{xy}^{*}
this follows from (
3.3.1
) and (
3.3.3
) for
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}^{*}
and from the fact that
\Gamma_{xy}^{*}:\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]\to\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]
is an algebra automorphism satisfying (
2.6.7
).

Now part (iii) immediately follows from (ii) as by (
3.3.19
)
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\gamma}=0
for any
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}
,
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
,
[\beta]<0
, so, in particular, for any
{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
one has
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{\beta}^{\delta_{{\bf m}}}=0
if
[\beta]<-1
, and also by (
2.6.9
)

(\Gamma_{xy}{\rm d}\Gamma_{y})^{\delta_{{\bf m}}}_{\beta}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq}0\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp}}(\Gamma_{xy})^{\delta_{{\bf m}}}_{\mu}({\rm d}\Gamma_{y})^{\mu}_{\beta}=0

if
[\beta]<-1
as
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{pp}\subset\mathcal{M}
and
({\rm d}\Gamma_{y})^{\mu}_{\beta}=0
in this case by part (ii).

∎

4.
Stochastic estimates for the model

In this section we state and prove the main result of this paper, stochastic estimates for the smooth models
(\Pi_{x},\Gamma_{xy}^{T})
constructed in Section
2
. The proof is quite complicated but follows the proof of a similar statement in
[
5
]
with some modifications. As we mentioned in the Introduction, the main difference comparing to
[
5
]
is that we obtain the stochastic estimates in a bounded range of the rescaling parameter which is one of the ingredients of them. This leads to some modification of the BPHZ condition imposed on the model in the course of the proof. The arguments in all statements related to estimates involving the BPHZ condition are also adjusted accordingly. In fact, these arguments are more close to the ones which appear in the proof of Proposition 5.2 in
[
25
]
.

After recalling the BPHZ condition in Section
4.1
and the translational invariance in law property for models in Section
4.2
we state the main result in Section
4.3
and then proceed with its proof.

4.1.
A generalized BPHZ condition

To obtain stochastic estimates stable under the limit
\rho\to 0
for the smooth model introduced in the previous section one has to fix the coefficients
c_{k}
,
k=1,2,3,4
in (
2.4.5
) in an appropriate way. This is achieved by imposing the so-called BPHZ condition. We say that a model
(\Pi_{x},\Gamma_{xy}^{T})
satisfies a generalized BPHZ condition with respect to a family of functions
\psi_{\beta}\in\mathcal{S}
,
\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,
|\beta|<2
if for all
\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,
|\beta|<2
one has

(4.1.1)

\mathbb{E}\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}(\psi_{\beta})=0,

where

\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}(\psi_{\beta})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}(x)\psi_{\beta}(x)dx.

For any given
\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with
|\beta|<2
we also say that
\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}
satisfies the generalized BPHZ condition with respect to
\psi_{\beta}
if (
4.1.1
) holds.

Proposition 4.1.1
.

For any family of functions
\psi_{\beta}\in\mathcal{S}
,
\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,
|\beta|<2
, which are even in the variables
x_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, there is a unique model of the form
(\Pi_{x},\Gamma_{xy}^{T})
which satisfies (
4.1.1
).

In fact, the unique model is completely determined by
\psi_{\beta}
for
\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}
,
k=1,2,3,4
, and for the other
\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,
|\beta|<2
the corresponding generalized BPHZ conditions are satisfied automatically.

In the proof of this proposition we follow the arguments of Section 1.12. in
[
5
]
.
We firstly describe the relevant multi–indices.

Lemma 4.1.2
.

The multi–indices
\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,
|\beta|<2
are of the form

(4.1.2)

\beta=k\delta_{\bf g},~|\beta|=k(\alpha+1)+\alpha=\frac{k-1}{2}-(k+1){\varepsilon},~k=0,1,2,3,4,5;

(4.1.3)

\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0},~|\beta|=k(\alpha+1)=\frac{k}{2}-k{\varepsilon},~k=1,2,3,4;

(4.1.4)

\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+2\delta_{\bf 0},~|\beta|=k(\alpha+1)-\alpha=\frac{(k+1)}{2}-(k-1){\varepsilon},~k=1,2,3;

(4.1.5)

\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{{\bf e}_{i}},~i=1,2,3,~|\beta|=k(\alpha+1)+1=\frac{(k+2)}{2}-k{\varepsilon},~k=1,2,

where

{\bf e}_{1}=(0,1,0,0),~{\bf e}_{2}=(0,0,1,0),~{\bf e}_{2}=(0,0,0,1).

Proof.

For
{\varepsilon}
in the range (
2.1.8
) formula (
2.1.5
) with
\alpha=-\frac{1}{2}-{\varepsilon}
for any
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
yields that either
\beta({\bf n})=0
for all
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
or

(4.1.6)

|\beta|\geq\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}})|{{\bf n}}|.

Thus, if
|\beta|<2
, in both cases
\beta({\bf n})=0
for
|{\bf n}|\geq 2
. By (
2.1.3
) the last condition implies that
\beta({\bf n})\neq 0
only for
{\bf n}\in\{{\bf 0},{\bf e}_{1},{\bf e}_{2},{\bf e}_{3}\}
.
Note that by (
4.1.6
)
\beta
may not vanish on a unique
{\bf e}_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
and it must take value
1
on it.

To estimate the value of
\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with
|\beta|<2
at
{\bf 0}
we observe that for
\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
one has
[\beta]\geq-1
which is equivalent by (
2.3.4
) to

(4.1.7)

\beta({\bf g})\geq\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}})-1.

Now we obtain from (
2.3.4
) using (
4.1.7
) that

2>|\beta|=(\alpha+1)\beta({{\bf g}})+\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}})(|{{\bf n}}|-\alpha)+\alpha\geq

\geq(\alpha+1)(\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}})-1)+\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}})(|{{\bf n}}|-\alpha)+\alpha=\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}})(|{{\bf n}}|+1)-1\geq\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}})-1

which implies

\sum_{{{\bf n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({{\bf n}})<3.

In particular,
\beta({\bf 0})<3
.

Using the last condition, recalling that
\beta
may not vanish on a unique
{\bf e}_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, and it must take value
1
on it, we deduce using (
2.1.5
) by examining all possible cases that any
\beta\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with
|\beta|<2
has one of the forms listed in (
4.1.2
)–(
4.1.5
).

∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1.1
.

Firstly we show that condition (
4.1.1
) is satisfied for
\beta
of types (
4.1.2
), (
4.1.4
) and (
4.1.5
) for any coefficients
c_{4}
,
k=1,2,3,4
.

Recall that
\xi^{\rho}(x)=\xi(\eta_{x}^{\rho})
,
\rho>0
, where
\eta\in\mathcal{S}
satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\eta(y)dy=1
,
\eta(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is even in the spacial coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
on the
\mathbb{R}^{3}
–factor in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and by
\xi(\eta_{x}^{\rho})
we mean that scalar components of
\xi
are evaluated at
\eta_{x}^{\rho}
.

Note that by the definition the covariance of the white noise
\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\xi_{i}dx_{i}
is invariant under translations of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, orthogonal transformations of the
\mathbb{R}^{3}
–factor in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and the transformation induced by the map
\xi\to-\xi
. These symmetries imply the following symmetries in law

(4.1.8)

-\xi^{\rho}=_{\rm law}\xi^{\rho},

(4.1.9)

R_{i}^{*}\xi^{\rho}=_{\rm law}\xi^{\rho},~i=1,2,3,

where
R_{i}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the reflection in the plane defined by the equation
x_{i}=0
,
i=1,2,3
, and
R_{i}^{*}
is the corresponding transformation of the differential 1-forms.

In fact symmetry (
4.1.8
) is induced by the canonical antiautomorphism
\tau
of
{\mathfrak{k}}
,
\tau z=-z
,
z\in{\mathfrak{k}}
. The action of this antiautomorphism on
{\mathfrak{k}}
naturally induces an invertible linear map of each
V_{\beta}
and
V_{\beta}^{-}
,
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
, and hence of
\mathcal{O}_{\beta}=\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\beta}
and of
\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}=\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}
which we denote by the same symbol
\tau
.

Applying
\tau
to (
2.4.4
), (
2.4.5
), (
2.4.8
), and (
2.4.9
) in the case of
\Pi_{0\beta}
and
\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}
we infer by induction over
\sum_{{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({\bf n})
, as in Section 1.12 of
[
5
]
, that symmetry (
4.1.8
) induces the following symmetries in law

(4.1.10)

(-1)^{1+\sum_{{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({\bf n})}\Pi_{0\beta}=_{\rm law}\Pi_{0\beta},~(-1)^{1+\sum_{{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({\bf n})}\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}=_{\rm law}\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}.

Similarly, each
R_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
induces a linear map of each
V_{\beta}
and
V_{\beta}^{-}
,
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
, and hence of
\mathcal{O}_{\beta}=\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\beta}
and of
\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}=\mathcal{O}\otimes V_{\beta}^{-}
which we denote by
R_{i}^{*}
.

Applying
R_{i}^{*}
,
i=1,2,3
to (
2.4.4
), (
2.4.5
), (
2.4.8
), and (
2.4.9
) in the case of
\Pi_{0\beta}
and
\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}
we infer by induction, as in Section 1.12 of
[
5
]
, that symmetry (
4.1.9
) induces the following symmetries in law

(4.1.11)

(-1)^{\sum_{{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}n_{i}\beta({\bf n})}R_{i}^{*}\Pi_{0\beta}=_{\rm law}\Pi_{0\beta},~(-1)^{\sum_{{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}n_{i}\beta({\bf n})}R_{i}^{*}\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}=_{\rm law}\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}.

The second symmetry in (
4.1.10
) immediately implies (
4.1.1
) for
\beta
of types (
4.1.2
) and (
4.1.4
).

For
\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{{\bf e}_{i}}
,
i=1,2,3
,
k=1,2
of type (
4.1.5
) one has from the second symmetry in (
4.1.11
) by the definition of
R_{p}^{*}
,
p=1,2,3

(4.1.12)

-R_{i}^{*}\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}=_{\rm law}\Pi_{0\beta}^{-},~R_{p}^{*}\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}=_{\rm law}\Pi_{0\beta}^{-},~p\neq i.

If
\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{{\bf e}_{i}}
,
i=1,2,3
,
k=1,2
then
V_{\beta}^{-}\simeq V\otimes V^{*}\simeq{\mathfrak{k}}\otimes{\mathfrak{k}}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where we identify
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}^{*}
using the fixed scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then
\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}\in\mathcal{O}_{\beta}^{-}\simeq\mathcal{O}\otimes{\mathfrak{k}}\otimes{\mathfrak{k}}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Denote by
(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{pq}
the
(p,q)
–component of the tensor
\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}
with respect to the basis
e^{p}\otimes e^{q}
,
p,q=1,2,3
of
\mathbb{R}^{3}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

In terms of the components
(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{pq}
the definition of
R_{p}
,
p=1,2,3
implies that the first symmetry in (
4.1.12
) yields

(4.1.13)

-(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{ii}(R_{i}x)=_{\rm law}(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{ii}(x),-(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{pq}(R_{i}x)=_{\rm law}(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{pq}(x),~p,q\neq i,

and from the second symmetry in (
4.1.12
) we obtain

-(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{pq}(R_{p}x)=_{\rm law}(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{pq}(x),~p\neq i,~p\neq q,

-(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{qp}(R_{p}x)=_{\rm law}(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{qp}(x),~p\neq i,~p\neq q,

so, in particular,

(4.1.14)

-(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{pi}(R_{p}x)=_{\rm law}(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{pi}(x),~-(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{ip}(R_{p}x)=_{\rm law}(\Pi_{0\beta}^{-})_{ip}(x),~p\neq i.

Since
\psi_{\beta}(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
are even in the spacial coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
, symmetries (
4.1.13
) and (
4.1.14
) imply (
4.1.1
) for
\beta
of type (
4.1.5
).

It remains to show that the inductive algorithm for defining
\Pi_{x\beta}
described in Section
2.4
along with condition (
4.1.1
) uniquely determine the model. Since for given coefficients
c_{p}
,
p=1,2,3,4
this algorithm combined with the results of Section
2.6
uniquely determines the model by the definition, it suffices to show that this algorithm is compatible with condition (
4.1.1
) and that they uniquely determine the coefficients
c_{p}
,
p=1,2,3,4
.

Indeed, formula (
2.4.5
) in the case of
\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}
implies that for any given
\beta
the recentered map
\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}
depends on
\Pi_{0\gamma}
with
\gamma({\bf g})<\beta({\bf g})
and
c_{p}
with
p\leq\beta({\bf g})
. Therefore for non–purely polynomial
\beta
with
|\beta|<2
,
\beta\neq 0
one can determine
\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}
and
\Pi_{0\beta}
by induction over
k:=\beta({\bf g})
with the help of (
2.4.5
) and (
2.4.9
).

In the course of application of this inductive algorithm over
k=1,2,3,4,5
, assuming that
\Pi_{0\gamma}^{-}
,
\Pi_{0\gamma}
and
c_{p}
are already known for
p<k
and
\gamma
with
\gamma({\bf g})<k
, we can firstly define
\Pi_{0k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}}^{-}
and
\Pi_{0k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}}
(if
k<5
) and then determine
\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}
and
\Pi_{0\beta}
for all the other
\beta
with
\beta({\bf g})=k
.

Note that in the case of
\Pi_{0k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}}^{-}
,
1\leq k<5
the term containing
c_{p}
,
p=1,2,3,4
in the right hand side of formula (
2.4.5
) takes the form

\sum_{l=1}^{k}c_{l}\Pi_{0(k-l)\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}}=c_{k}+\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}c_{l}\Pi_{0(k-l)\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}}

as
\Pi_{0\delta_{\bf 0}}=1
by the definition. Therefore
\Pi_{0k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}}^{-}-c_{k}
depends on
c_{l}
,
l<k
and
\Pi_{0\gamma}
with
\gamma({\bf g})<k
, which are already known by the induction assumption. Hence condition (
4.1.1
) holds for
\beta=k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}
provided that
c_{k}
is given by the formula

c_{k}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}(\Pi_{0k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}}^{-}(x)-c_{k})\psi_{k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}}(x)dx,

where the expression
\Pi_{0k\delta_{\bf g}+\delta_{\bf 0}}^{-}-c_{k}
is known by the induction assumption.

Thus the inductive algorithm for defining
\Pi_{x\beta}
described in Section
2.4
is compatible with condition (
4.1.1
) and they uniquely determine the coefficients
c_{p}
,
p=1,2,3,4
and the model as well. This completes the proof.

∎

4.2.
Translational invariance in law

Another important property of the recentered maps
\Pi_{x\beta}(y)
,
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}(y)
and of the structure group maps
\Gamma_{xy}
, which is crucial in the proof of the stochastic estimates for them, is translational invariance in law stated in the next proposition.

Proposition 4.2.1
.

The recentered maps
\Pi_{x\beta}(y)
,
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}(y)
and the structure group maps
\Gamma_{xy}
are invariant in law with respect to translations in the sense that for all
x,y,h\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
\beta\in{\bf Mult}
one has

(4.2.1)

\Pi_{x\beta}(y)=_{\rm law}\Pi_{x+h\beta}(y+h),~\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}(y)=_{\rm law}\Pi_{x+h\beta}^{-}(y+h),

(4.2.2)

\Gamma_{x+hy+h}=_{\rm law}\Gamma_{xy}.

Proof.

By the definition the covariance of the white noise,
\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\xi_{i}dx_{i}
is invariant under translations in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
This symmetry implies the following symmetry in law

(4.2.3)

\xi^{\rho}(x+h)=_{\rm law}\xi^{\rho}(x),~x,h\in\mathbb{R}^{4}.

Applying translation by
h
to both arguments
x
and
y
in (
2.4.4
), (
2.4.5
), (
2.4.8
), and (
2.4.9
) in the case of
\Pi_{x\beta}(y)
and
\Pi_{x\beta}^{-}(y)
and using (
4.2.3
) we infer by induction over
\sum_{{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}\beta({\bf n})
, as in Section 1.12 of
[
5
]
, that symmetry (
4.2.3
) induces symmetries (
4.2.1
).

To justify (
4.2.2
) we restrict
\Gamma_{xy}^{*}
to
\mathbb{R}_{n}:=\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}_{\bf g},\{{\bf z}_{\bf m}^{i}:i=1,\ldots,{\rm dim}(V),{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},|{\bf m}|<n\}]]
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and use induction over
n
.

If
n=0
,
\mathbb{R}_{0}=\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}_{\bf g}]]
. By (
2.6.7
) the restriction of
\Gamma_{xy}^{*}
to
\mathbb{R}_{0}=\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}_{\bf g}]]
satisfies

\Gamma_{x+hy+h}^{*}=\Gamma_{xy}^{*},~x,y,h\in\mathbb{R}^{4}.

This establishes the base of the induction.

Now assume that the restriction of
\Gamma_{xy}^{*}
to
\mathbb{R}_{n}
satisfies (
4.2.2
) for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
be such that
|{\bf n}|=n
.
Observe that by (
4.2.1
) and by (
3.3.13
)
Y_{\bf n}(x+h)({\bf z})=_{\rm law}Y_{\bf n}(x)({\bf z})
, and hence by (
3.3.13
), by (
2.6.6
) and by the induction assumption

\Gamma_{xy}^{*}({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf n}}=\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}\Gamma_{xy}^{*}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{x}(x)({\bf z})-\Gamma_{xy}^{*}Y_{\bf n}(x)({\bf z})=

=\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{y}(x)({\bf z})-\Gamma_{xy}^{*}Y_{\bf n}(x)({\bf z})=_{\rm law}\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{y+h}(x+h)({\bf z})-\Gamma_{x+hy+h}^{*}Y_{\bf n}(x+h)({\bf z})=

=\frac{1}{{\bf n}!}\Gamma_{x+hy+h}^{*}\partial^{\bf n}\Pi_{x+h}(x+h)({\bf z})-\Gamma_{x+hy+h}^{*}Y_{\bf n}(x+h)({\bf z})=\Gamma_{x+hy+h}^{*}({\rm Id}_{W_{\delta_{\bf n}}}^{V}\otimes{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{R}[[{\bf z}]]}){\bf z}_{\delta_{\bf n}},

where at the last step we used (
3.3.13
) again.

Repeating the same arguments for all
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
with
|{\bf n}|=n
we deduce that
the restriction of
\Gamma_{xy}^{*}
to
\mathbb{R}_{n+1}
satisfies (
4.2.2
). This establishes the induction step and completes the proof.

∎

4.3.
The main statement

Now we are in a position to state the main result of the paper.
For this purpose observe that by Lemma
2.3.1
the set
\{|\beta|_{\prec}:\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}\}\subset I+\frac{5}{2}\mathbb{N}
is locally finite and bounded from below, so that the set
\mathcal{M}_{N}:=\{|\beta|_{\prec}:\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0},~|\beta|_{\prec}<N\}
is finite for any
N\in\mathbb{R}
.

Let
n_{0}<n_{1}<\ldots<n_{M(N)}
be the elements of the set
\mathcal{M}_{N}
, and for
\bar{\lambda}_{0}>0
,
k=0,\ldots,M(N)
denote
\mathcal{M}_{k}:=\{\beta:\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0},~|\beta|_{\prec}=n_{k}\}
,
\bar{\lambda}_{k}:=\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{0}}{32^{k}}
.

Theorem 4.3.1
.

For any given mollifying parameter
\rho>0
, any
N\geq 17
and
\bar{\lambda}_{0}>0
satisfying
\bar{\lambda}_{M(N)}=\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{0}}{32^{M(N)}}\geq 1
there exists a smooth model
(\Pi_{x},\Gamma_{xy}^{T})
which satisfies a generalized BPHZ condition and such that for all
1\leq p<\infty
,
\gamma,\mu\in\mathcal{M}
the following inequality holds

(4.3.1)

\left\|(\Gamma_{xy})^{\gamma}_{\mu}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\lesssim|x-y|^{|\mu|-|\gamma|},

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
if
\mu\not\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}
, and if
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{k}
,
k=0,\ldots,M(N)
it holds for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
|x-y|\leq\bar{\lambda}_{k}
.

Also, for all
1\leq p<\infty
,
\gamma,\mu\in\mathcal{M}
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}
,
r>{\rm max}\{2-\alpha,|\mu|\}
the following inequalities hold

(4.3.2)

\left\|\Pi_{0\mu}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\lesssim\lambda^{\alpha-2}(\lambda+|x|)^{|\mu|-\alpha},

(4.3.3)

\left\|\Pi_{0\mu}(\varphi_{x}^{\lambda})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\lesssim\lambda^{\alpha}(\lambda+|x|)^{|\mu|-\alpha}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
0<\lambda<\infty
if
\mu\not\in\mathcal{M}_{\geq 0}
, and if
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{k}
,
k=0,\ldots,M(N)
they hold for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
|x|\leq\bar{\lambda}_{k}
,
0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{k}
.

The constants in inequalities (
4.3.1
), (
4.3.2
) and (
4.3.3
) do not depend on
\lambda
,
\varphi
,
x
,
y
and
\rho
.

If
N\geq 17
the set
\{\beta\in\mathcal{M}:|\beta|_{\prec}<N\}
contains all
\beta\in\mathcal{M}
such that
|\beta|\leq 2
.

This theorem is proved by induction over the modified homogeneity. The proof will be presented in Section
4.5
. In the next section we obtain the desired estimates in the case of the base of this induction when
\mu=0
which will be used in the proof of Theorem
4.3.1
.

4.4.
The base case

Firstly, for completeness and for referencing purposes we recall some algebraic properties of
\Gamma_{xy}
,
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}
and
S_{x+yx}
which are nearly trivial in the base case.

Proposition 4.4.1
.

For all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
one has
(\Gamma_{xy})_{0}^{\gamma}=\delta_{0}^{\gamma}{\rm Id}_{W_{0}}
.

Proof.

As by (
2.6.2
) and (
2.6.3
)
\Gamma_{xy}:W_{0}\to W_{0}
is the identity transformation, and
W_{0}=\mathbb{R}
, we deduce
(\Gamma_{xy})_{0}^{\gamma}=\delta_{0}^{\gamma}{\rm Id}_{W_{0}}
.

∎

Proposition 4.4.2
.

For
\gamma\neq\delta_{\bf n}
,
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
one has
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{0}^{\gamma}=0
.

Proof.

By (
3.3.4
)
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{0}^{\gamma}=0
if
\gamma\neq\delta_{\bf n}
for some
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
.

∎

Proposition 4.4.3
.

For
\gamma\neq\delta_{\bf n}
,
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
one has
(S_{x+yx})^{\gamma}_{0}=0
.

Proof.

Note that by (
3.3.4
)
({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{0}^{\gamma}=0
if
\gamma\neq\delta_{\bf n}
for some
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
. Using this fact and (
2.6.8
) we deduce that
(S_{x+yx})^{\gamma}_{0}=0
if
\gamma\neq\delta_{\bf n}
for some
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
,
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}
.

∎

Next, we proceed with a series of estimates for Malliavin derivatives. We start with the following elementary basic result.

Proposition 4.4.4
.

For any
1\leq q<2
,
\bar{\lambda}_{0}>0
and
\upsilon\in\mathbb{L}_{q}(L_{2})
satisfying
\left\|\left\|\upsilon(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq 1
one has

(4.4.1)

\left\|\delta\Pi_{00}^{-}\right\|_{q\bar{\lambda}_{0}0}^{\alpha-2}=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{0}}\frac{\left\|\delta\Pi_{00}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}}{\lambda^{\alpha-2}}\lesssim 1,

where
\delta=\delta_{\upsilon}
and the constant in the inequality only depends on
\bar{\lambda}_{0}
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\Pi_{00}^{-}(x)=\xi^{\rho}(x)=\xi(\eta^{\rho}_{x})
, so by the Hölder and by the Young convolution inequalities we have for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{0}

\left\|\delta\Pi_{00}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}=\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq\left\|\left\|\upsilon(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\left\|\eta^{\rho}_{0}(x)\right\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\left\|\varphi^{\lambda}_{0}(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq

(4.4.2)

\leq\lambda^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left\|\left\|\upsilon(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\left\|\varphi(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\lesssim\lambda^{-\frac{d}{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{\alpha-2},

for
0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}
as

\left\|\left\|\upsilon(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq 1

by the assumption,

\left\|\eta^{\rho}_{0}(x)\right\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}=1,

\left\|\varphi(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\lesssim 1
, and
-\frac{d}{2}>\alpha-2=-\frac{d}{2}-{\varepsilon}
.
This completes the proof.

∎

Now using Schauder estimates for
\mathcal{K}
we can derive an estimate for
\delta\Pi_{00}
.

Proposition 4.4.5
.

For any
1\leq q<2
,
\bar{\lambda}_{0}\geq 1
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\geq 1
and
\upsilon\in\mathbb{L}_{q}(L_{2})
satisfying
\left\|\left\|\upsilon(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq 1
one has

\left\|\delta\Pi_{00}\right\|_{q\bar{\lambda}_{0}r}^{\alpha}=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}}\frac{\left\|\delta\Pi_{00}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}}{\lambda^{\alpha}}\lesssim 1,

where
\delta=\delta_{\upsilon}
and the constant in the inequality only depends on
\bar{\lambda}_{0}
and
r
.

Proof.

Since by (
2.4.4
)
\Pi_{00}
depends on
\xi
polynomially, one obviously has from (
2.4.4
)

\delta\Pi_{00}=\mathcal{K}\delta\Pi_{00}^{-}.

Observe also that
r\geq 1>|\alpha|={\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{2}
.
Hence, the estimate in the statement follows from (
4.4.1
), and from (
3.2.5
) with
x=0
, and
F=\delta\Pi_{00}^{-}
.

∎

Next, we proceed with a Besov type estimate for a germ.

Proposition 4.4.6
.

For any
\bar{\lambda}_{0}\geq 1
,
R>0
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\geq 2
,
1\leq q<2
,
\upsilon\in\mathbb{L}_{q}(L_{2})
with
\left\|\left\|\upsilon(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq 1
the germ

F_{x}(y):=\delta\Pi_{00}(y)-\sum_{\gamma\in{\bf Mult}}\Pi_{x\gamma}(y)\circ({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{0}^{\gamma}

satisfies

(4.4.3)

\left\|F\right\|_{q2\bar{\lambda}_{0}Rr}^{\alpha+\frac{d}{2}}=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\frac{\left\|\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}}\left\|F_{x}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\right\|_{L_{2}(B_{R},x)}}{\lambda^{\alpha+\frac{d}{2}}}\lesssim 1,

where
\delta=\delta_{\upsilon}
, and the constant in the inequality only depends on
\bar{\lambda}_{0}
and
r
.

Proof.

By (
3.3.8
)

F_{x}(y)=\delta\Pi_{00}(y)-\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}:\\
|{\bf n}|<2-{\varepsilon}\end{array}}\Pi_{x\delta_{\bf n}}(y)\circ({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})_{0}^{\delta_{\bf n}}.

From this observation and (
3.3.9
) we deduce that for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}

F_{x}(y)
is obtained from
\delta\Pi_{00}(y)
by subtracting its Taylor polynomial at
x
of parabolic degree
1
. Thus
F_{x}=(\delta\Pi_{00})_{x}^{2-{\varepsilon}}=(\mathcal{K}\upsilon^{\rho})_{x}^{2-{\varepsilon}}
, and we can apply (
3.2.10
) with
b=-{\varepsilon}
,
r>2-{\varepsilon}
to get

(4.4.4)

\left\|F\right\|_{q2\bar{\lambda}_{0}B_{R}r}^{2-{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}\right\|_{q2~2\bar{\lambda}_{0}B_{R}r}^{-{\varepsilon}}\leq\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}\right\|_{q2~2\bar{\lambda}_{0}\mathbb{R}^{4}r}^{-{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}\right\|_{\mathbb{B}_{q2}^{-{\varepsilon}}},

where at the last step we used definition (
3.1.4
).

Now we estimate the right hand side using definition (
3.1.6
) of the space
\mathbb{B}_{q2}^{-{\varepsilon}}
according to which

(4.4.5)

\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}\right\|_{\mathbb{B}_{q2}^{-{\varepsilon}}}\lesssim\sup_{0<\lambda\leq 2\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\frac{\left\|\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}(\varphi_{x}^{\lambda})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}}{\lambda^{-{\varepsilon}}},

where
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}
is such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\varphi(x)dx\neq 0
.
Since
{\varepsilon}>0
, this definition implies

(4.4.6)

\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}\right\|_{q2~2\bar{\lambda}_{0}B_{R}r}^{-{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}\right\|_{\mathbb{B}_{q2}^{-{\varepsilon}}}\lesssim\sup_{0<\lambda\leq 2\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\left\|\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}(\varphi_{x}^{\lambda})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}.

As
1\leq q<2
we can proceed further by the Minkowski and by the Young convolution inequalities,

\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}\right\|_{q2~2\bar{\lambda}_{0}B_{R}r}^{-{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\sup_{0<\lambda\leq 2\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\left\|\left\|\upsilon^{\rho}(\varphi_{x}^{\lambda})\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq

(4.4.7)

\leq\sup_{0<\lambda\leq 2\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\left\|\left\|\upsilon\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\left\|\eta^{\rho}\right\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\left\|\varphi^{\lambda}\right\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\lesssim\left\|\left\|\upsilon\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq 1,

where we also used the fact that by the definition
\left\|\eta^{\rho}\right\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}=\left\|\eta\right\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}=1
,
\left\|\varphi^{\lambda}\right\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}=\left\|\varphi\right\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}
does not depend on
\lambda
, and that by the assumption
\left\|\left\|\upsilon\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq 1
.

Note that the last chain of inequalities resembles the Besov space inclusion
L_{2}\subset B_{2\infty}^{-{\varepsilon}}
.

Finally (
4.4.3
) follows from (
4.4.4
) and from (
4.4.7
) given that
2-{\varepsilon}=\alpha+\frac{d}{2}
. This completes the proof.

∎

Now we can obtain an estimate for
\Pi_{x0}^{-}
using the previously obtained estimate for its Malliavin derivative and the spectral gap inequality.

Proposition 4.4.7
.

For any
\bar{\lambda}_{0}>0
, and any
1\leq p<\infty

(4.4.8)

\left\|\Pi_{x0}^{-}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}_{0}0}^{\alpha-2}=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{0}}\frac{\left\|\Pi_{x0}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}}{\lambda^{\alpha-2}}=

=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{0}}\frac{\left\|\Pi_{00}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}}{\lambda^{\alpha-2}}\lesssim 1

where the constant in the inequality only depends on
\bar{\lambda}_{0}
and
p
.

Proof.

By translational invariance in law it suffices to consider again the case
x=0
, and by the Hölder inequality in probability one can restrict to the case when
p>2
. Also, the equality in the right hand side of (
4.4.8
) is obvious as
\Pi_{x0}^{-}=\xi^{\rho}
does not depend on
x
.

We shall use spectral gap inequality (
3.1.7
) for
f=\Pi_{00}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0})
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{0}
.
Since the underlying Gaussian measure is centered,
\mathbb{E}(\Pi_{00}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0}))=\mathbb{E}(\xi^{\rho}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0}))=0
, and the spectral gap inequality takes the form

\left\|\Pi_{00}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\lesssim\sup_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\upsilon\in\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}(L_{2}):\\
\left\|\left\|\upsilon\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}}\leq 1\end{array}}\left\|\delta_{\upsilon}\Pi_{00}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq

\leq\sup_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\upsilon\in\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}(L_{2}):\\
\left\|\left\|\upsilon\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}}\leq 1\end{array}}\left\|\delta_{\upsilon}\Pi_{00}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}},

where
1\leq q\leq p^{*}<2<p
,
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{*}}=1
, and at the last step we used the Hölder inequality in probability.

Thus by Proposition
4.4.4
with
q=p^{*}
for
0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}
one has

\left\|\Pi_{00}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\lesssim\lambda^{\alpha-2},

where the constant in the inequality only depends on
\bar{\lambda}_{0}
and
p
. This implies (
4.4.8
).

∎

Finally, using Schauder estimates for
\mathcal{K}
we can derive an estimate for
\Pi_{x0}
.

Proposition 4.4.8
.

For any
\bar{\lambda}_{0}\geq 1
,
r\geq 1
and any
1\leq p<\infty

(4.4.9)

\left\|\Pi_{x0}\right\|_{p\bar{\lambda}_{0}r}^{\alpha}=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}}\frac{\left\|\Pi_{x0}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}}{\lambda^{\alpha}}=

=\sup_{0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}}\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}}\frac{\left\|\Pi_{00}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}}{\lambda^{\alpha}}\lesssim 1,

where the constant in the inequality only depends on
\bar{\lambda}_{0}
,
p
and
r
.

Proof.

By translational invariance in law, the estimate in the statement follows from (
4.4.8
) and from (
3.2.5
) with
b=|0|-2=\alpha-2
and
F_{x}=\Pi_{x0}^{-}
as in this case by (
2.4.9
)

\Pi_{x0}=\mathcal{K}\Pi_{x0}^{-}
.

∎

The last two propositions in this section stated below will be used at the next step of the inductive proof of Theorem
4.3.1
.

Proposition 4.4.9
.

For any
1\leq q<2
,
\bar{\lambda}_{0}>0
,
0<R\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}
and
\upsilon\in\mathbb{L}_{q}(L_{2})
with
\left\|\left\|\upsilon(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq 1
one has

(4.4.10)

\left\|({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})^{\delta_{\bf n}}_{0}\right\|_{q2B_{R}x}\lesssim R^{\frac{d}{2}+\alpha-|\delta_{\bf n}|},

where
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}
is defined with the help of
\upsilon
, and the constant in the inequality only depends on
\bar{\lambda}_{0}
. The left hand side of the inequality vanishes if
|{\bf n}|\geq 2-{\varepsilon}
.

The vanishing property in Proposition
4.4.9
follows from Proposition
4.4.2
.
The proof of estimate (
4.4.10
) will be given after the proof of Proposition
4.9.7
.

Proposition 4.4.10
.

For any
1\leq q<2
,
\bar{\lambda}_{0}>0
,
R\in\mathbb{R}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
0<R,|y|\leq\bar{\lambda}_{0}
,
{\bf n}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
and
\upsilon\in\mathbb{L}_{q}(L_{2})
with
\left\|\left\|\upsilon(x)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},x)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}\leq 1
one has

(4.4.11)

\left\|(S_{x+yx})^{\delta_{\bf n}}_{0}\right\|_{q2B_{R}x}\lesssim|y|^{\frac{d}{2}+\alpha-|\delta_{\bf n}|},

where
S_{x+yx}
is defined with the help of
\upsilon
, and the constant in the inequality only depends on
\bar{\lambda}_{0}
. The left hand side of the inequality vanishes if
|{\bf n}|\geq 2-{\varepsilon}
.

The vanishing property in Proposition
4.4.10
follows from Proposition
4.4.3
.
The proof of estimate (
4.4.11
) will be given after the proof of Proposition
4.9.8
.

4.5.
The induction step statement

In this section we formulate the induction step statement required for the proof of Theorem
4.3.1
.
We start by introducing a function which will be used to impose a BPHZ condition.

For any function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}
and any
\lambda>0
we denote by
\varphi^{\lambda}(y):=\varphi^{\lambda}_{0}(-y)
the function
\varphi
rescaled by
\lambda
. We also denote by
\ast
the convolution of functions.

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{D}
be an even function such that
{\rm supp}(\omega)\subset B_{\frac{1}{6}}
, and

(4.5.12)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\omega(x)dx=1,~\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}x^{\bf m}\omega(x)dx=0,~0<|{\bf m}|\leq r-1,~{\bf m}\in\mathbb{N}^{4},

where
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
r>2-\alpha
. Such function exists, e.g., by Lemma 8.1 in
[
13
]
.

Define the function

(4.5.13)

\psi:=\omega^{2}\ast\omega,

where
\omega^{2}
is
\omega
rescaled by
2
.

Proposition 4.5.11
.

Let
\rho>0
be the mollification parameter in the definition of smooth models
(\Pi_{x},\Gamma_{xy}^{T})
in Section
2
with recentered maps
\Pi_{x\mu}
,
\Pi_{x\mu}^{-}
, and structure group maps
\Gamma_{xy}
. Let
\rho>0
be arbitrary, and let
\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}}{8}\geq 1
, and
\beta\in\mathcal{M}
,
\beta\neq 0
.

Suppose that for all
1\leq p<\infty
,
\gamma,\mu\in\mathcal{M}
with
|\mu|_{\prec}<|\beta|_{\prec}
one has

(4.5.14)

\left\|(\Gamma_{xy}\right)^{\gamma}_{\mu}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\lesssim|x-y|^{|\mu|-|\gamma|},~x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{4},~|x-y|\leq\bar{\lambda}_{\prec},

where the constant in the inequality is independent of
x
and
y
and
\rho
.

Assume furthermore that for any
1\leq p<\infty
,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
with
|\mu|_{\prec}<|\beta|_{\prec}
, and any
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{r}
for some (or for any)
r>{\rm max}\{2-\alpha,|\mu|\}
one has

(4.5.15)

\left\|\Pi_{0\mu}^{-}(\varphi^{\lambda}_{x})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\lesssim\lambda^{\alpha-2}(\lambda+|x|)^{|\mu|-\alpha},~x\in\mathbb{R}^{4},~|x|\leq\bar{\lambda}_{\prec},~0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{\prec},

and

(4.5.16)

\left\|\Pi_{0\mu}(\varphi_{x}^{\lambda})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\lesssim\lambda^{\alpha}(\lambda+|x|)^{|\mu|-\alpha},~x\in\mathbb{R}^{4},~|x|\leq\bar{\lambda}_{\prec},~0<\lambda\leq\bar{\lambda}_{\prec},

where the constants in the inequalities do not depend on
\lambda
,
\varphi
,
x
and
\rho
.

If
|\beta|<2
, assume in addition that the recentered map
\Pi_{0\beta}^{-}
satisfies the generalized BPHZ condition with the function
\psi^{\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}}
, and that for all
\gamma,\mu\in\mathcal{M}
with
|\mu|_{\prec}<|\beta|_{\prec}
,
1\leq q<p^{*}<2
,
{\rm d}\Gamma_{x}
and
S_{x+yx}
defined with the help of
\upsilon\in\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}(L_{2})
such that
\left\|\left\|\upsilon\right\|_{L_{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p^{*}}}\leq 1
one has

(4.5.17)

\left\|({\rm d}\Gamma_{x})^{\gamma}_{\mu}\right\|_{q2B_{R}x}\lesssim R^{\frac{d}{2}+|\mu|-|\gamma|},~0<R\leq\bar{\lambda}_{\prec},

and

(4.5.18)

\left\|(S_{x+yx})^{\gamma}_{\mu}\right\|_{q2B_{R}x}\lesssim

\lesssim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|y|^{\frac{d}{2}-|\gamma|_{p}+\alpha}(|y|+R)^{|\mu|-|\gamma|+|\gamma|_{p}-\alpha}&{\rm if}~\frac{d}{2}-|\gamma|_{p}+\alpha>0\\
(|y|+R)^{|\mu|-|\gamma|+\frac{d}{2}}&{\rm else}\end{array}\right.,~0<R,|y|\leq\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}}{2},

where the constants in the inequalities are independent of
\rho
,
R
,
\upsilon
and
y
.

Then if
|\beta|<2
(resp.
|\beta|\geq 2
) estimates (
4.5.14
), (
4.5.15
), (
4.5.16
), (
4.5.17
) and (
4.5.18
) (resp. (
4.5.14
), (
4.5.15
), (
4.5.16
)) hold with
\mu
replaced by
\beta
,
\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}
replaced by
\bar{\lambda}=\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}}{32}
, and with the same other conditions as in the assumptions, where
\mu
is replaced by
\beta
, and
\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}
is replaced by
\bar{\lambda}
.

Remark 4.5.12
.

By Lemma 8.1 in
[
13
]
, given any smooth compactly supported function
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
with non–vanishing integral over
\mathbb{R}^{4}
one can find a linear combination of a finite number of rescaled
\varphi
which satisfies (
4.5.12
). Therefore the BPHZ condition in Proposition
4.5.11
can be imposed with respect to a function from a larger class. To simplify exposition we prefer to fix
\omega
satisfying (
4.5.12
) from the very beginning.

Remark 4.5.13
.

In the statement of Proposition
4.5.11
when
|\beta|\geq 2
one can actually put
\bar{\lambda}=\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}}{8}
. For uniformity of the statement we define
\bar{\lambda}=\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}}{32}
for all
\beta\in\mathcal{M}
.

Strategy of the proof of Proposition
4.5.11
.

We outline the strategy of the proof which will be presented as a series of statements in Sections
4.6
,
4.7
,
4.8
and
4.9
. The conditions in each statement in these sections depend on the results obtained in the preceding statements only and on the induction assumptions imposed in this proposition. However, claims in Sections
4.6
,
4.7
,
4.8
and
4.9
are often stated under weaker assumptions and sometimes results stronger than those required for the induction step are proved.

Estimates (
4.5.14
), (
4.5.17
) and (
4.5.18
) for
\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}
replaced by
\bar{\lambda}
,
\mu=\beta
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{pp}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
are established in Propositions
4.6.1
,
4.6.2
and
4.6.3
, respectively, (see also Corollary
4.6.4
for the exact statement), and for
\mu=\beta
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{pp}
they are obtained in Propositions
4.9.3
,
4.9.7
, and
4.9.8
, respectively (see Corollaries
4.9.4
and
4.9.9
for the exact statement).

Estimate (
4.5.15
) for
\bar{\lambda}_{\prec}
replaced by
\bar{\lambda}
,
